  - ⚠ **SHARED WHERE EXACT, PER-WINDOW WHERE NOT, and the first version got this wrong and shipped.** A shared capture hides every frame, so it is the desk; a window sitting on another then showed blurred *desk* where the window beneath should be — a hole punched through the stack. The resolution is that a shared capture is **exact** for any window whose rectangle overlaps no lower window, so only real overlaps need their own — none in the tiled layout. ⚠ Overlap must be **strictly positive**: tiled windows abut, and a plain `intersects` counts a zero-width touch. ⚠ **The tiled layout is the wrong one to check stacking in**, which is why this went unseen: every render was tiled. `-Ddeck.cascade` on the harness fixes that.
  - ⚠ **24fps is a CEILING, paced against `UiTokens.FROST_BUDGET`** — each refresh is measured and the next is spaced by `cost / budget`, so the frost stays correct at any window count and only its frequency degrades (full rate tiled, ~7fps cascaded). A fixed rate would hand the thread to the blur exactly when the player has the most on screen. ⚠ It reads `System.nanoTime`, the one place the "always the session clock" rule inverts: this measures how long *this machine* took, and a wound clock would report a 34ms capture as instant.
  - ⚠ **Reduced motion stops the clock** and the event-driven path takes over, so the frost is still correct after every interaction and never moves on its own.
  - ⚠ **THREE GROUNDS, and the middle one was missed first time round.** `-es-panel` is the window body (glass); **`-es-well`** is anything sunk into a panel — terminal scrollback, table bodies, text fields, the cycle grid, the map canvas, the calculator — and **aliases `-es-void`** so the opaque palettes are untouched; `-es-float` is anything floating over content, opaque. ⚠ `-es-void` was doing two unrelated jobs (the desk AND every recess), and painting a well with it **punched a black box through the glass** in six tools. In the glass palettes a well is a *tint over the frost*: darker than the panel, and more opaque than it, because a terminal's content sits directly on it.
  - ⚠ **TRANSMISSION IS 80% (dark) / 68% (light) WITH THE BLUR BEHIND IT**, roughly twice what was survivable without one, and `ContrastTest.transmissionRequiresFrost` is what couples the two — a palette this transmissive that stopped declaring `frostsBackdrop()` would put a sharp, fully readable second screen under every window. The bounds below are the record of what transparency costs UNFROSTED, and the constraint any future unfrosted palette is still held to.
  - ⚠ **BEFORE THE BLUR: every bound was found by rendering, none by arithmetic.** Three failures, in order: (1) at 12% the desk substrate's rows of hex came through every panel as horizontal **banding**, indistinguishable on the light palette from a failing display — it slips under a bound written for *text* because the content is *texture*; (2) at 64% the notification stack over the LOG window was two columns of text in the same pixels; (3) desk windows overlap at all, so without blur a translucent panel shows the one beneath it **sharp**.
  - ⚠ **THE FILM MUST BE LIGHT, AND THAT IS PHYSICS RATHER THAN TASTE.** A frosted pane scatters *additively* — it lifts what is behind it toward grey rather than tinting it darker, which is why a macOS glass panel over a black desktop is a mid grey. Measured at one alpha and desk: a mid-toned film leaves the text behind at **4.95:1** (a readable second screen), a light film at **2.03:1** (present, not readable). The authentic choice turned out to be the only survivable one. ⚠ The cost is that the dark palette's panel composites to a mid graphite and its greys are near-whites, so the ramp is compressed — which is again what the reference actually looks like rather than a compromise.
  - ⚠ **THE NUMERIC BOUND IS NECESSARY AND NOT SUFFICIENT, and this is the finding worth keeping.** The 64% build measured **2.78:1** against a 3:1 floor and passed comfortably; on screen each text was individually below the legibility threshold and the pair was unreadable, because **a per-pair luminance ratio cannot see two texts competing for the same glyph cells**. `ContrastTest.whatShowsThroughIsNotReadable` holds the floor and the shipped palettes sit well under it. **Passing that test does not mean a palette is legible.**
  - ⚠ **`-es-float` — a WINDOW may be glass; a thing that FLOATS over content may not.** Toasts, dialogs, context menus, tooltips, the download dock, the sync banner. It **aliases `-es-panel-hi`** (verified on JavaFX 26: a looked-up colour may reference another, and a theme may override it), so the six opaque palettes change in no way and only the glass ones override it with an opaque literal. A window body may transmit because what is behind it is usually the desk; an alert is over content by definition, and an alert you cannot read is not an alert.
  - ⚠ **The ghost bound was WEAKENED to make this possible, deliberately.** It was "no more legible than greeble" — the stronger claim, and the one to restore if the direction changes — which caps transmission near 5%. It is now "below the floor at which this client considers a thing legible". What that gives up is stated in `ContrastTest`: the backdrop is no longer merely a material, and a player who overlaps two windows will see shapes under the top one.
  - ⚠ **A SIX-DIGIT REGEX SILENTLY TRUNCATES AN EIGHT-DIGIT COLOUR, AND THAT WOULD HAVE BEEN WORSE THAN NO CHECK.** `ContrastTest` matched `#[0-9A-Fa-f]{6}`; against `#RRGGBBAA` it does not fail — it takes the first six digits and drops the alpha, so every contrast assertion in the client would have measured text against a panel colour that is never on screen and reported a pass. The class composites now: panel over the desk, raised surface over the **panel** (a header strip sits inside a window, so two glass layers stack).
  - ⚠ **The accent stays WARM AMBER**, on explicit direction, though the reference is unmistakably blue. §2.1 calls the warm-on-cool temperature split load-bearing, and a blue accent standing beside the existing `gain`/`warn`/`alarm` is the semantic colour system §2.1 bans, arriving one token at a time. The material is the reference's; the vocabulary is the game's.
  - ⚠ **These themes round windows without writing the player's §9.3 setting** — a costume has to come off cleanly. `ThemeId.cornersAreRounded` is the one place that knows the rule, because the two call sites that shape a window (the Scene-root clip and the desk frames) would otherwise be one edit from disagreeing. It reuses the existing `.es-rounded` class rather than a theme-scoped radius, so §9.3's machine-checked "never round a measurement" keeps protecting it with no second rule to maintain — and `UiContractTest` needed **no** amendment.
  - ⚠ **`UiContractTest`'s sheet lists were hand-kept and had already drifted**: the cursor check named five of the six sheets that existed, so `theme-cyberdeck.css` was exempt from a build-blocking rule by clerical accident. Both lists are derived from `ThemeId` now, and the no-blur/no-shadow scan was widened from `theme.css` alone to **every** sheet — a palette overlay being exactly where "just a touch of blur to sell the glass" would land.
  - ⚠ **The render harness had to be taught the feature or it would have photographed the wrong thing.** `DeckSnapshot` selects a theme but never re-applied the geometry, so every liquid frame came out **square** — the failure mode this repo records repeatedly: a harness capturing the one state indistinguishable from the feature being absent, and reporting it as a pass.
  - ⚠ **The first palette was tuned against the deck's desk-to-panel step and was wrong.** That step is nearly invisible, which is correct for an interface of hairlines with no fills and wrong for glass, **where the lift *is* the material**. A pane level with its ground is not a pane.
- 2026-08-04 — **Daily stock, purchase noise, and the store branded GROUP OF HACKS** — `rules/{MarketStock,SaveMarketStock}`, `Balance.MARKET_FOREIGN_PURCHASE_NOISE_CYCLES`, `view/MarketView`.

  ⚠ **STOCK IS WORLD STATE, NOT CHARACTER STATE, AND THAT IS WHY IT IS A PORT.** On a server the shelf is shared — a run on a discounted item is meant to be felt by everybody — so a counter in `GameSave` would give each player a private shelf, which is the exact opposite of a limited edition. `MarketStock.Held` is the seam: `SaveMarketStock` in solo (one player, so per-character and per-world coincide, and the behaviour matches LAN by construction), the server's own table online (**W-7**).
  ⚠ **The RATION is derived from (item, day); only the COUNT TAKEN is stored.** Same rule as the deals — a drawn quantity would restock the shelf on every repaint and "3 left" would mean nothing. Storing the remaining level instead would be a second copy of a derived number that disagrees the first time the ration is re-tuned.
  ⚠ **Stock is the sink a discount is not.** A deal lowers the price, taking ethecoin *out* of the sink; scarcity raises urgency without touching the price, which is the pressure a shop actually wants and it costs the economy nothing. That asymmetry is why the discount bands are narrow and the stock bands are not. **Consumables are stocked deep (6–14), permanents shallow (1–3)** — a consumable is bought over and over, so a low ration reads as the shop being broken; a permanent is bought once, and one on the shelf is a genuine race. ⚠ **An item on offer is stocked shorter but never to zero**: a deal nobody can buy is worse than no deal.
  ⚠ **A gated item is NOT STOCKED, which is not zero.** "0 in stock" says come back tomorrow; a gated item is never coming. The wire type therefore omits the key rather than sending 0, and the card shows the gate instead.
  ⚠ **Purchase noise rides on the download TASK**, so it is present-tense and ends by itself — `NoiseRules` already counts a running task's declared loudness, so this needed no new mechanism and cannot leave a rig permanently loud. **Own home server: silent** (the same reasoning as **I9** — you do not surveil yourself). **LAN, foreign federated, and solo: loud**, because the vendor is somebody else's machine. ⚠ The `foreign` parameter defaults to **true**, the loud direction: an unconverted caller being quietly free is a stealth bug nobody can see.
  ⚠ **The balance shows two decimals under the TOP STRIP'S LICENSED EXCEPTION, not a new one.** The rule is that a held amount is never rounded — a rounded amount somebody holds is a lie they cannot detect — sharpened to "may be abbreviated only where the exact figure is one hover away". So `formatApprox(wei, 2)` plus a tooltip plus `accessibleText`, and the LEDGER stays exact. ⚠ `formatApprox` appends the unit itself; a second `" EC"` rendered **"500 EC EC"**, caught by rendering.
  **Branding and layout:** GROUP OF HACKS, a scrollable body, a search field over name/description/**tags** (every offering now carries them), and stock counts on every card. ⚠ **Deliberately NO account-level nav row** — the reference layout's STORE/LIBRARY/COMMUNITY bar was struck out in the request, and correctly: the deck already carries identity and balance in its top strip and navigation on its rail, so a second one inside a window would be the same navigation twice. ⚠ **The deals strip is not filtered by the search** — a search asks "show me what matches"; the strip answers "what is worth buying today", and hiding an offer for not matching a typed word would make the shop look empty. ⚠ **An empty section says so rather than vanishing**, or a search that matches nothing looks like the shop lost a category.

  ✅ Green: 970 protocol + 527 engine + 596 client unit, 207 integration.

- 2026-08-04 — **The market is a storefront with rotating deals, and the discount ceiling is ARITHMETIC rather than taste** — on explicit direction; `engine/{Durability}`, `engine/rules/MarketDeals`, `protocol/game/MarketWindow`, `client/view/MarketView`.

  ⚠ **THE FINDING THAT SHAPED THE WHOLE FEATURE: a discount past ~26% turns the economy's only sink into a faucet.** Everything ethecoin-gated is resellable (`Repac.sellable`), and `Repac.resaleValue` is a fraction of the **catalogue** price rather than of what was paid. A market package ships at `MARKET_UPGRADE_VERSION_MAJOR` (3), so it fetches `RESALE_PERCENT` (60) scaled by `UPGRADE_VERSION_RESALE_PERCENT_PER_MAJOR` (12) per major — **74.4% of retail**. Buy at 30% off and sell it straight back and that is **+4.4% of retail per cycle, with no compute cost, no thermal recovery and no cap**, which is `docs/design/00` §4's meta-rule (compute is the master scarcity) broken directly. The request already said "no deals that are too good"; what was not knowable without reading `Versions.resaleWei` is that the ceiling is a specific number and that exceeding it is not generosity but an unbounded ethecoin generator.
  ⚠ **So it is DERIVED, not chosen.** `MarketDeals.breakEvenDiscountPercent()` computes it from those three constants and `maxDiscountPercent()` holds five points below; every band clamps through `bounded()`. A re-tune of the resale percentage moves the ceiling automatically. `MarketDealsTest` walks **a year of rotations across five characters** and asserts no deal and no bundle ever reaches break-even — in percentage *and* in wei, because rounding is where a compliant percentage still produces an under-water price.
  **Bands: consumables 10–20%, permanents 5–10%, bundles 12–18%.** ⚠ Permanents are shallower not because they are stronger — **I2** already forbids anything here being a ceiling — but because a permanent is bought **once**: the discount leaves the sink permanently smaller for a decision the player was going to make anyway, where a consumable's is spent again next time. ⚠ **A bundle's rate is no deeper than a consumable's**: the saving comes from the number of items, and a 40%-off bundle of two permanents is two 40%-off permanents as far as `Repac.sell` is concerned.
  ⚠ **`Durability` is a new classification and is explicitly NOT a gate.** Nothing already on `Offering` answered it, and `equippedCycles == 0` is wrong in both directions — a Net Sweep holds nothing and is kept forever, a Relay hop holds nothing and is per-session. The convenience constructor defaults to **PERMANENT**, the cautious direction: an entry added without thinking gets the smaller sale, never the deepest discount in the game by omission.
  ⚠ **Derived from (character, 3-day epoch), never drawn and never stored.** The storefront repaints on a clock and a drawn deal would reshuffle the shelves every second — `MempoolRules.projectionDepth`'s rule. `floorDiv`, so an instant before 1970 does not share a window with one after it. Seeded on the character too, so two players are not looking at one shop.
  ⚠ **Only ethecoin-gated items are eligible.** A "sale" on a schematic-gated item would put a price on the one thing whose whole point is that it has none — I2, and the most misleading thing the panel could say. Gated items keep their cards and their explanations, under a **NOT FOR SALE** heading.
  ⚠ **A wire type, not a direct rules call.** The view reads `GameSession.market()`; the engine runs on a server for LAN and federated play, so a view calling `MarketDeals` would render a shop in single player and an empty shelf online. `RemoteGameSession` returns `MarketWindow.none()` — an empty shelf rather than catalogue prices with no deals, which would be a claim that session cannot make.
  ⚠ **Rendering it caught a clock bug.** `MarketWindow` now carries `asOf` — the session's clock — because a countdown built on `Instant.now()` reported "8h 5m" against a window the game clock had opened minutes earlier. `CLAUDE.md`'s standing rule about deadlines, found again by [[feedback-verify-by-rendering]].
  ⚠ **The bundle is priced but not purchasable in one action**, and the card says so rather than offering a button that half-works: a loop over `purchase()` would charge retail per item and silently ignore the bundle discount. Open as a follow-up.

  ✅ Green: 970 protocol + 517 engine + 596 client unit, 207 integration.

- 2026-08-04 — **CLIENT LOGS: the LOG window's third tab, and logging across the client** — `client/log/{ClientLog,LogEntry,LogLevel}`, `view/ClientLogView`, `LogTab.CLIENT`.

  ⚠ **`java.util.logging`, and no new dependency.** SLF4J would have meant widening the client's enforcer ban for a diagnostic. JUL is in the JDK and — more usefully — is what the libraries already here log through, so **one handler captures the client and its libraries in a single ordered stream**, which is the version of this panel worth opening.
  ⚠ **THE ROOT LOGGER IS NOT OPENED, AND THE MEASUREMENT IS THE ARGUMENT.** Setting the root to `ALL` is the obvious way to "capture everything" and it makes the panel useless inside one frame: **JavaFX logs its own layout at `FINEST`** — a record per node resized and per node moved, per pass — and the first render dropped **11,905 records**, filling the buffer with `Region@2b0d7119[styleClass=increment-arrow] moved to (10.0,0.0)` and evicting every line the client had logged. ⚠ **It compiled and every capture test passed.** Only rendering it showed the defect, which is [[feedback-verify-by-rendering]] holding again. `io.github.stoicswe.eyeandsickle` goes to `ALL`; the root keeps its default so libraries still contribute INFO and above; `-Deyeandsickle.log.verbose=true` opens it for anyone chasing a library. `ClientLogTest.theRootIsLeftAlone` is the guard, because the wrong version passes every other test in the file.
  ⚠ **TRACE is CAPTURED but not SHOWN**, and the split is the feature. A player asked to turn trace on sees what led *up* to the problem rather than only what happens after they flip the switch — otherwise every trace investigation opens with "reproduce it again", which for an intermittent fault means "we cannot help you".
  ⚠ **Five bands over JUL's nine.** FINER/FINEST collapse into TRACE and CONFIG folds into DEBUG: four toggles that never change what is on screen would teach a reader the filter does not work. Banded by `intValue()` rather than identity, so a library's **custom** Level (Flyway and Jackson both define one) is not silently dropped.
  ⚠ **Per-level toggles, not a minimum severity.** A threshold cannot express "warnings and errors only, without the info chatter", which is the first view anybody wants when something has gone wrong.
  ⚠ **A `LogRecord` is flattened at capture**, on the logging thread. It is mutable and reusable, its message is a format string resolved later, and its parameters may be live game objects — so holding one renders somebody else's message when the panel repaints, and pins an object graph meanwhile.
  ⚠ **Installed in `Launcher.main`, before the toolkit**: no backfill exists, and start-up is when the failures worth sending in happen.
  ⚠ **Two-class CSS selectors** (`.list-cell.es-log-error`), because `.list-cell` already sets `-fx-text-fill` at one-class specificity — the trap `CLAUDE.md` records. All five colours are existing `ContrastTest` tokens and therefore measured in six palettes; **`-es-dim-3` was NOT available** for a dimmer trace line, being the greeble token exempt from the floor, which is exactly how the network map's nodes once vanished.
  ⚠ **The `ListCell` clears every level class before adding one** — cells are recycled, and a class left behind paints an INFO line in the error colour.
  **Instrumentation is at CHOKEPOINTS**, the rule the event bus already follows: `changed()`/`announce()` (FINE for a success, INFO for a refusal — a refusal is rarer and is what somebody is looking for), `Shell.finish()` as the single exit every command returns through, the two disk writes, `DeskManager.open/close`, `LocalDatabase`'s migration with its applied count, and every previously-silent catch. `InstrumentationTest` proves the client actually logs, separately from `ClientLogTest` proving the buffer works — adding a logger field and a call compiles whether or not the line is on a path anything takes.

  ✅ Green: 970 protocol + 506 engine + 596 client unit, 207 integration.

- 2026-08-04 — **The REST API is documented, and generating the docs found that the REST API answers 401 to everything** — new doc [`../architecture/14-api-documentation.md`](../architecture/14-api-documentation.md); springdoc-openapi 3.1.0, `server/web/OpenApiConfiguration`, `OpenApiSpecIT`.

  ⚠ **THE FINDING MATTERS MORE THAN THE FEATURE.** `spring-boot-starter-security` has been on the classpath since the identity slice, and **no `SecurityFilterChain` bean existed anywhere**, so Boot's default `anyRequest().authenticated()` applied: sign-in, LAN join, the session transport and the entire federation surface all return **401**. **Nothing noticed because no test in the server module has ever made an HTTP request to one of its own controllers** — every integration test is repository- or service-level, and the only `RestClient` in the test tree is an *outbound* peer transport. The 401 surfaced the first time anything fetched a URL from this server, which was the new spec test. ⚠ **Scoped deliberately**: `ApiDocsSecurityConfiguration` carves out the doc paths and preserves the existing behaviour for everything else. Which endpoints are public, which need an allowlisted account and which need a verified service-auth JWT is the application's authentication model, and settling it in the permissive direction as a side effect of adding documentation would open a server meant to be closed. Recorded as **API-1**, and it belongs with CL-8 — note that the real mechanism is not HTTP Basic at all but the service-auth JWT `identity/ServiceAuthVerifier` already checks.
  ⚠ **`@DynamicPropertySource` CANNOT SET `spring.profiles.active`, and `ServerContextLoadsIT` had been lying about its coverage because of it.** Profiles resolve while the `Environment` is being prepared, before dynamic properties are contributed, so the registry form is accepted, ignored, and reported only as `No active profile set, falling back to 1 default profile` — in a log nobody reads in a passing test. That class asserted it exercised the federation beans and migrations while running neither; `@ActiveProfiles("federation")` fixes it, and the context still loads, so the coverage that was claimed is now real.
  ⚠ **Both surfaces ship OFF** (`EYEANDSICKLE_API_DOCS`, `EYEANDSICKLE_SWAGGER_UI`), following the server's existing posture rather than inventing one: the allowlist starts empty and LAN mode refuses a public address, so an interactive request builder answering whoever can reach the port would sit badly beside that. **Two switches, not one** — the spec is a static document naming paths and shapes, the console issues live requests, and an operator handing a developer the schema should not have to expose a request builder to do it.
  ⚠ **The spec is CHECKED, not merely produced.** `OpenApiSpecIT` asserts it both ways — every mapped path documented, every documented path really mapped — reading the mapped set from `RequestMappingHandlerMapping` rather than a hand-kept list. **Verified by hiding a controller**: `@Hidden` on `LanJoinController` fails with `/api/lan/join is mapped but absent from the spec`. The test enables docs for itself, which is what lets the shipped default stay closed *and* the spec stay checked.
  ⚠ **springdoc 3.x, not 2.x** — 2.x is built against Boot 3; 3.1.0 is built against Boot 4.1.0 exactly. A 2.x on Boot 4 resolves cleanly and fails at runtime. ⚠ **The version comes from Boot's `build-info`**, injected as an `ObjectProvider` because the bean is absent when the packaging goal has not run: a hard dependency would turn "documentation metadata is missing" into "the server does not start".

  ✅ Green: 972 protocol + 506 engine + 583 client unit, 207 integration (+2). Opens **API-1** (the security policy), **API-2** (per-endpoint prose) and **API-3** (whether a release should publish the spec).

- 2026-08-03 — **Every piece of legacy-format support removed** — on explicit direction, on the stated premise that the current build is the first anyone will play. Nothing has shipped, so no save, settings file or database predates it, and code that reads an older format is reading a format that has never existed.

  Removed: the JSON save importer (`JsonSaveImport`, `CharacterSlots.saveFile`, four import tests); the **26 `@JsonAlias` pre-wei keys** across eight state classes plus `GameSave.moneySchema` and `GameEngine.rescaleMoney`; `LegacySaveTest`; the **ten setter-only `@JsonProperty` hooks** for pre-per-character appearance and the `ringChromatic` rename hook, with their test nest and the rule policing them; `GameEngine.retuneNetworkHashrate`; and the four patch migrations **V3–V6, folded into V2**.

  ⚠ **WHAT WAS DELIBERATELY KEPT, because it is not legacy support and the two are easy to confuse.** Every money field keeps its **zero initialiser** — a null-safety rule, and the one that `ContributionState.creditedWei` lacked when it threw an NPE on the login screen. `GameSave.format` / `CURRENT_FORMAT` stay: refusing a save written by a *newer* build is FORWARD compatibility, which is the opposite of the thing being deleted. And `Slot.problem` still reports an unreadable character, because a corrupt row is not a legacy format — it is a hand-edited database or a half-flushed page, both of which can happen tomorrow.
  ⚠ **The squash was PROVEN equivalent, not eyeballed.** A schema dump — every column with its type, nullability, default and generation expression; every constraint, check clause, foreign-key rule, index column, trigger and routine; and the seeded singleton — taken before and after and diffed. **Columns, constraints, checks, FKs, triggers, routines and seed data are byte-identical.** The single difference is one **redundant engine-generated index** on `deployed_miners.host_rig_id`: H2 indexes the foreign key itself, and in the old chain V6's `ALTER COLUMN ... SET DATA TYPE` rebuilt the table and consolidated the two. The named index is kept because it says what the access path is *for* — attributing a deployed miner to the rig whose compute it spends, Invariant I6's read — and an engine-generated name documents nothing.
  ⚠ **Two tests were quietly asserting the migration mechanism rather than the schema, and the squash exposed it.** `SchemaVocabularyTest` replayed the later ALTERs over V2's declarations to compute the "effective" column types; with the replay gone, its column regex stopped at the word `numeric` and dropped the `(78,0)` — which would have let a **bigint ethecoin column** through while still reporting a pass. That is the 9.22 EC ceiling this project has already been bitten by. `CharacterSlotsMigrationTest` asserted the text of V3's `ALTER`s and is now `CharacterSlotsSchemaTest` against V2's declarations, with the dead backfill check dropped and the uniqueness check strengthened to assert `uq_players_did` is *absent* as well as `uq_players_did_slot` present.
  ⚠ **`theBaselineIsNotRewritten` WAS DELETED, AND IT MUST COME BACK.** It forbade precisely the V2 edit this squash performs, and it was correct: a migration that has been applied anywhere is frozen by its checksum, so a tidied baseline fails every existing deployment on startup with a message that names nothing useful. It is wrong only for exactly as long as there are no deployments. **Restore it with the first release.**

  ✅ Green: 972 protocol + 506 engine + 583 client unit, 205 integration.

- 2026-08-03 — **`solo` renamed to `engine`** — the module has been the game's only rules engine since 2026-08-02 and has owned the shared save system since earlier today, so the name had been wrong twice over and was actively misleading: it read as "the single-player one", which is precisely the duplication that no longer exists. Directory, artifact (`eyeandsickle-engine`), package (`io.github.stoicswe.eyeandsickle.engine`), and the two classes carrying the same wrong word — `SoloGame` → `GameEngine`, `SoloSave` → `GameSave`.
  ⚠ **"Solo" AS A PLAY MODE IS A REAL CONCEPT AND WAS DELIBERATELY LEFT ALONE.** `SessionMode.SOLO`, `soloSlots`, `soloHandle`, `startSolo` and the legacy `solo-save.json` filename all describe *how somebody is playing*, which is a true and useful distinction. Only the ones naming the *module* were wrong. ⚠ `soloHandle` is also a persisted settings key and `solo-save.json` is the file `JsonSaveImport` looks for — renaming either would silently orphan a real player's data, which is the trap a blanket search-and-replace walks straight into.
  ⚠ **`EngineSessions` moved to `engine/session/`, not `engine/engine/`** — the mechanical rename produced the doubled package and it compiled as far as the server before anything complained.
  ✅ Green after the rename: 972 + 509 + 592 unit, 205 integration. The narrowed enforcer ban was re-negative-tested (adding `spring-web` to the renamed module still fails the build), because a rule that stops firing during a rename is a rule nobody notices is gone.

- 2026-08-03 — **ONE SAVE SYSTEM: solo and the server share the store, the schema and the engine host** — on explicit direction, after a feasibility pass that recommended *against* the storage half and was overruled. The recommendation was not wrong about the arithmetic and the direction was right about the goal: uniformity is worth more than the ~120 lines the split was costing, because the cost of two stores is paid by whoever has to remember which one they are looking at.

  ⚠ **THE PAIR WAS WRITING IDENTICAL BYTES, which is the fact that makes this a simplification rather than a trade.** `character_game_state.state` is `text` holding the engine's own JSON document, deliberately opaque to SQL (V7's comment: a query reaching inside it would be a *second* way to read game state, able to disagree with the first, invisibly). So `FileSaveStore` and `JdbcSaveStore` differed in **container only** — and the split bought two atomicity stories, two failure modes and two places for the save format to drift.
  ⚠ **A THIRD MIGRATION TIER, `engine`, and it is the only one single player runs.** `V7` creates `character_game_state` with **no foreign key**; core's new `V8` adds the reference to `players` where there is an authority model to reference. Requiring the FK in the engine tier would drag eighteen authority tables, the DID alias and the append-only triggers into a mode that can use none of them. ⚠ V7 sits *between* core's V6 and V8 on purpose — Flyway shares one history across locations and applies in version order, and it does not require the versions it applies to be contiguous, so a server gets 1…6,7,8,1001,1002 and single player gets exactly 7.
  ⚠ **`EngineSessions` moved to the engine module and takes FUNCTIONS.** Load → tick → act → persist, one engine per character, one request at a time — the ordering was enforced on the server and a *convention* in the client, because the only correct host lived behind a container the client is forbidden to have. It needs neither Spring nor JDBC now; `ServerEngineSessions` is 40 lines of wiring.
  ⚠ **The enforcer bans were NARROWED, not dropped, and the message says why.** H2, Flyway and spring-jdbc are permitted; **Spring Boot, `spring-web` and the server module are not**. The distinction is not how much Spring — it is whether the module could start listening on a port. A driver is a place to put bytes; the thing the rule protects is that server code never reaches the client.
  ⚠ **I14 IS UNTOUCHED, AND THE FULL ARGUMENT IS WORTH WRITING OUT BECAUSE IT IS EASY TO GET BACKWARDS.** What made a player-editable save safe was never the file format. **A local H2 file is exactly as editable as JSON was**, merely less pleasant to read, so nothing may ever be trusted on the grounds that it came out of a database — and equally, changing the container cannot have weakened anything. What holds the line is that a solo character has **no route to a server**, which is mechanical in two places: (1) it has no DID and no `players` row, so going online means a character created *on* a home server via `CharacterService` — allowlist, cap, starts empty — and the one export path that exists is guarded by `Quarantine.refuseIfLan`; (2) ⚠ **core's `V8` makes `character_game_state.character_id` a foreign key to `players`**, so a forged engine-state row has nowhere to land on a server until an authorised player exists. The engine tier deliberately omits that constraint (V7 has no FK) and single player never runs V8 — the asymmetry *is* the design: the engine's state has one shape everywhere, and only the authority tier says who may own some. `SchemaIT.engineStateRequiresAPlayer` and `deletingAPlayerCascadesToEngineState` pin both halves, verified by removing the constraint first.
  ⚠ **WHAT WAS GIVEN UP, PLAINLY.** The save is no longer human-readable, diffable, or mailable to a bug report, and `FileSaveStore`'s own comment defended those as deliberate. That is a real loss with no replacement, accepted for uniformity. `JsonSaveImport` reads a pre-2026-08-03 `solo-save*.json` once into the database and **leaves the file in place** — an import that eats its input can only ever be run once, correctly, forever.

  **Two live bugs fell out of doing it, both of which would have shipped:**
  ⚠ **A FILE H2 WITH NO POOL CLOSES BETWEEN EVERY STATEMENT AND LOSES WRITES.** `JdbcDataSource` opens a connection per statement and closes it, so at H2's default `DB_CLOSE_DELAY=0` the database is opened and closed between operations. Measured: a row written through one call was visible to the next read and **absent from the one after that** — present at `readSlot(1)`, gone by `readSlot(2)`, with nothing in between but the loop. It presents as a phantom deletion and it is not one. `DB_CLOSE_DELAY=-1`. The server never saw it because Hikari holds connections, which is also why nothing caught it before a second, pool-less caller existed.
  ⚠ **THE SERVER'S SHIPPED JDBC URL COULD NOT CONNECT AT ALL.** `AUTO_SERVER=TRUE` and `DB_CLOSE_ON_EXIT=FALSE` are mutually exclusive in H2 — a hard refusal at connect time, SQLState 50100 — and `application.yml` carried **both** from the PostgreSQL migration. A self-hoster running the boot jar would have met it on the first launch. **No test caught it because every test uses an in-memory URL**, which is the general lesson: an integration suite that never opens the *production* kind of database is not testing the production configuration.
  ⚠ **And one pre-existing client bug**: `CharacterSlots.readSlot` passed `getMessage()` straight into the slot's `problem` field, and a `null` message made `unreadable()` false — so an exception rendered the slot as **empty**, which is precisely the failure its own comment says that branch exists to prevent ("a slot that reads as empty invites the player to overwrite the character they were trying to recover").

  ✅ Green: 972 protocol + 509 engine + 588 client unit tests, and 203 integration tests. The four legacy-import regression tests were **verified against neutered code first** — an import guard removed, then the legacy-file deletion removed — because a regression test that passes both ways reports the bug as fixed.

- 2026-08-03 — **The `-Pit` suite is green (203/203), and finishing it found that the H2 schema was BROKEN IN PRODUCTION** — the assumption logged the day before, that the 147 failures were Postgres-specific SQL written inside the tests, was right about roughly half of them and **wrong about the half that mattered**. Four defects were in the schema or in main code, and every one of them would have shipped.

  ⚠ **H2 2.4.240 (what the Boot 4.1 BOM brings) makes EVERY CHECK CONSTRAINT IN THIS SCHEMA PERMANENTLY DEAD.** It evaluates a check against the session that *parsed* it — `ConditionInConstantSet` keeps the parsing `SessionLocal` as its `TreeSet` comparator — so once Flyway closes its migration connection, every insert into `players`, `items`, `ledger_transactions`, `breach_resolutions` and `provenance_records` fails. Upstream: [h2database#4291](https://github.com/h2database/h2database/issues/4291) (dup #4292, #4320, #4342), no released fix, 2.4.240 is the newest release. **Pinned to 2.3.232** in `server/pom.xml`, which a locally-declared `dependencyManagement` entry can do against an imported BOM.
  ⚠ **The error accuses the wrong thing, which is why this was not found earlier.** SQLState **23514** is "check constraint *invalid*", not 23513 "*violated*": the constraint did not reject the row, it could not run — and H2 **drops the cause**, so the message names a constraint the row satisfies perfectly. It is not an `IN`-list problem either; `a = 'x' OR a = 'y'` and `n IN (1,2,3)` fail identically. ⚠ **A file database re-parses its constraints on open**, so the bug presents as intermittent: broken from the migration until a restart, fine afterwards — and with a connection pool it is worse still, working until Hikari evicts Flyway's connection on `idleTimeout` and then stopping.
  ⚠ **`?::jsonb` is legal in H2 and means something ELSE.** `CAST('{"a":1}' AS JSON)` produces the JSON *string* `"{\"a\":1}"`, one scalar whose content looks like an object. Nothing fails at the cast; the column's shape constraint fails several frames later naming `ck_items_attrs_object` against a document the caller can see is an object. Forty rows. The spelling that parses is `? FORMAT JSON` (SQL standard) — `Jsonb.CAST`.
  ⚠ **`~` runs a JAVA regex, so `[^[:space:]]` is not a POSIX class.** Java reads it as "not one of `:` `s` `p` `a` `c` `e`", so `ck_federation_peers_endpoint` and `ck_character_directory_endpoint` refused **every** URL rather than only malformed ones. Verified both ways before and after: `\S` accepts `https://peer.example.test` and `http://localhost:8080` and still refuses `ftp://`, an embedded space and `notaurl`. **Same failure shape as the check-constraint bug and as the `jsonb_typeof` regex the day before: syntactically valid, semantically inverted, silent.**
  ⚠ **A partial UNIQUE index lost its `WHERE` and became a different RULE.** `uq_flagged_servers_active` was `... WHERE cleared_at IS NULL` — "one *live* flag per server". H2 has no partial index, so the port dropped the predicate, turning it into "one flag per server, ever": a server flagged and then cleared could **never be flagged again**. Expressed now as a generated column that is the DID while live and NULL once cleared, since a unique index treats NULLs as distinct. ⚠ Only the *unique* one was a semantic loss — the other five partial indexes are non-unique, where dropping the predicate is a performance change.
  ⚠ **`ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` is NOT `MERGE ... KEY (...)`.** That is an upsert; measured, it overwrote the row that was supposed to be left alone. The equivalent is a `MERGE` with **no `WHEN MATCHED` branch**, which also preserves the 0-row return that `Mutations.requireInserted` and `AllowlistRepository.insertIfAbsent` both read as "already present". `INSERT ... RETURNING` became `SELECT ... FROM FINAL TABLE (INSERT ...)` — SQL:2011, one statement, so the schema defaults still come from the row the engine wrote rather than being predicted, and no concurrent writer can get between an INSERT and a follow-up SELECT.
  ⚠ **`ServerContextLoadsIT` was failing on something with nothing to do with the database**: `EngineSessions` and `GameSessionService` each had two unannotated constructors, and Spring cannot choose — the whole context died with "No default constructor found", which names neither the class's problem nor the fix. Both now take the application's `@Primary Clock` bean and have exactly one.
  ⚠ **One bug was introduced DURING this pass and caught by these tests**: casting a `bytea` parameter through `varchar` in the new `MERGE` statements UTF-8-decoded it, so every byte ≥ 0x80 became U+FFFD and a 32-byte transport key read back as 64 bytes of replacement characters — silently. The first symptom in production would have been a federation peer whose signatures never verify.
  ⚠ **Two stale TESTS were fixed rather than deleted, and one had been passing for the wrong reason.** `SchemaIT` still used the puzzle class `logic`, which `V4` replaced with `breach_protocol`/`offset_cipher`; the tier-range assertion passed only because H2 happened to evaluate the tier check before the class check. Fixtures now read `EnumColumns.PUZZLE_CLASS_VALUES`. `migrationsApply` asserted an exact list of version numbers, so it failed on every migration added — it now asserts the properties that actually matter (nothing recorded as failed, both ranges present, federation sorting above core). ⚠ **The ledger and account fixtures were seeding pre-wei hundredths** and are now converted through `Ethecoin`, so a fixture cannot drift from the column's scale again.
  ⚠ **The FK is NAMED now (`fk_provenance_records_item`).** An unnamed `REFERENCES` gets an engine-generated name — `provenance_records_item_id_fkey` in Postgres, `CONSTRAINT_66` in H2 — so a test asserting on it was asserting the vendor rather than the rule.
  ✅ **`PostgresSaveStore` → `JdbcSaveStore`, `PostgresIntegrationTestBase` → `DatabaseIntegrationTestBase`**, done after the suite was green so the rename did not hide the real fixes in review.
  ⚠ **The lesson for the next port is that "the migrations apply cleanly" proves almost nothing.** All 106 statements applied, on a build that was green, while three constraints were inverted or inert and a fourth had changed meaning. Every one of these is invisible to a unit test because every one is a property of the database rather than of the code that talks to it — which is the argument for keeping `-Pit`, now that it costs nothing to run.

- 2026-08-02 — **W-6: who is the AT Proto OAuth client** — resolved to **Option C: the desktop client runs the flow, the home server verifies independently** — recorded in [`../architecture/10-oauth-and-did-resolution.md`](../architecture/10-oauth-and-did-resolution.md) §1, which is now tagged decided for that section. The client is a **public** OAuth client; the server does **not** take its word for the DID, and verifies a short-lived **service-auth JWT** (`com.atproto.server.getServiceAuth`, `aud` = the home server's DID) against the `#atproto` verification method in a DID document it resolves itself. **Option A was rejected on the self-hosting tax:** `client_id` must exactly equal the URL its metadata document is fetched from, so a server-as-client design gives every self-hosted home server a mandatory public domain, certificate and keypair — a tax on the one thing (`docker compose up` on a spare box) that is supposed to be easy, and `deploy/` had never said so. **Option B was rejected as a shipping design** because a server accepting a client's bare assertion of "I am this DID" is a forgeable sign-in in a game whose social layer runs on informants, dossiers and an evidence threshold (`12-identity-and-social.md`) — it survives only as a flag-gated intermediate stage, disabled by default, which is what the flag is for. ✅ **Option C reuses W-1**: the DID→document resolver it needs is the same one item provenance needs, so half of W-1 closes in the same pass. ⚠ **Its one dependency is not finished shipping** — obtaining a service-auth JWT needs the granular `rpc:` scope, which Bluesky reports live on `bsky.social` and *rolling out* to the self-hosted PDS distribution, with explicit advice not to ship on it yet; `10` §7 therefore stages the verification leg last. ⚠ **I14 is not violated but is now load-bearing in a new place:** tokens and a DPoP keypair live on the player's machine, and I14 governs *game state*, not credentials — so `10` §4.4's rule that none of it may ever reach `ClientProfile` is the thing holding the line. ⚠ **Public-client sessions cap at two weeks** by spec; a fortnight away means signing in again, and that should be said in the UI rather than discovered.

- 2026-08-02 — **Operator logging, for LAN and federated alike** — `server/audit/OperatorLog`, wired at the sign-in, LAN-join and intent **chokepoints** rather than at call sites, which is the same rule the client's `EventBus` follows and for the same reason: coverage cannot drift as callers are added. Answers the three questions an operator has without a debugger — who is on my server, what are they doing, what did I refuse and why. ⚠ **Players log as `username@rig`.** The username is a display name the player chose and handed over, exactly like a federated handle; it is safe to log and it is what an operator actually wants. ⚠ **THE UUID IS NEVER LOGGED.** A LAN identity is `did:easlan:<uuid>` and that UUID is a **bearer token** — logging it would make the operator's log file a list of passwords, in a file they tail, ship to aggregators and paste into bug reports. An un-named identity falls back to a short stable fingerprint so no line is unattributable, and a **federated DID is logged in full** for the opposite reason: it is a public identifier that proves nothing on its own, and fingerprinting it would destroy correlation with the allowlist, ledger and directory for no gain. ⚠ **Usernames are sanitised** — a newline in a player-supplied name would otherwise let them forge log lines, including a fabricated denial for somebody else. ⚠ **Levels mean something**, because `alert-fatigue(7)` is a page in this game's own manual: INFO for normal events, WARN for a refusal the operator can act on (an allowlist denial is the system *working*), ERROR reserved for a replayed token — the one routine event that means somebody is trying. ⚠ **The name registry is bounded**: the key is attacker-supplied, since anyone who can reach a LAN server can join repeatedly.

- 2026-08-02 — **PostgreSQL removed; the server embeds H2** — on explicit direction, after an investigation that ran the real migrations against H2 rather than reasoning about them. `../architecture/01` §2 amended. **Why it is a win:** a self-hoster needs a JVM and nothing else — no database to install, no container, no connection string — and `../architecture/03` §1 calls "anyone can run a server" a core requirement. ⚠ **The investigation's first answer was wrong and measurement corrected it.** Estimated "a day, mostly mechanical"; the real blockers were only ~5 root causes behind 107 failures (64 were cascades), but two of them were the enforcement layer. ⚠ **PL/pgSQL guards became Java triggers** (`persistence/AppendOnlyTrigger`, `MonotonicSequenceTrigger`) — they still fire INSIDE the engine on every write, so the guarantee `08` §2 depends on survives, but they are our code now and earn the original's scrutiny. ⚠ **`is_did` came out BETTER**: an H2 ALIAS onto `Did.isWellFormed`, so the CHECK constraint and the Java validator are ONE implementation where the code previously carried a warning that two copies must be kept in step. ⚠ **H2 is a COMPILE dependency, not a driver** — the schema's enforcement is written against its API, so the server's code is coupled to H2, not merely configured for it. ⚠ **THREE traps found by testing rather than review.** (1) A single regex rewrote every `jsonb_typeof(x) = 'array'` into an object check — six constraints silently inverted, nothing failing at migration time; caught by counting object/array constraints against the pristine originals, and the fix was to revert and redo type-aware. (2) An H2 alias parameter typed `Object` — the defensive-looking choice — fails at constraint *evaluation* with SQLState **23514** ("check constraint invalid") rather than 23513 ("violation"): the constraint did not reject the row, it failed to run. A check that cannot execute protects nothing, and the two states differ by one digit. It must be `String`. (3) H2 has no `USING` clause on a type change, so the wei rescale became a separate `UPDATE` — and the ORDER matters, since multiplying before widening overflows the bigint being left. ⚠ **Proven, not assumed:** `is_did` refuses a malformed DID; the append-only trigger refuses both UPDATE and DELETE on the ledger; and a 30-digit wei value round-trips exactly, which is the 9.22 EC trap this project has already been bitten by once. ✅ **Bonus: the integration tests no longer need Docker.** They were behind `-Pit` because "a real database" meant a real PostgreSQL; 203 of them now run wherever the build does. ⚠ **They did not yet pass at this point** — 147 failed, and the assumption recorded here, that those were Postgres-specific SQL *inside the tests*, **turned out to be wrong for the most serious of them**. See the 2026-08-03 entry.

- 2026-08-02 — **ONE ENGINE, TWO STORES — the server now runs the real rules** — on explicit direction, and it is a better answer than either option I had offered. I had framed it as *rewrite the engine server-side* (Route A) versus *host solo's engine in the server* (Route B); the third framing — **extract the seam so both drive one engine** — is strictly better and should have been on the table. ⚠ **The refactor was small because the seam already existed**: `SaveStore` was a two-method class over one `GameSave` root, so it became an **interface** with `FileSaveStore` (a JSON file, single player) and `JdbcSaveStore` (the same state in the server's own database) behind it. `GameEngine` is unchanged. ⚠ **`CLAUDE.md`'s standing warning that solo is "a SECOND IMPLEMENTATION of a subset of the rules" is now FALSE and has been amended** — there is one engine, so a `design/03` re-tune lands in every mode at once and cannot drift. ⚠ **I14 is satisfied rather than bent**: the engine runs on the server against server-held state; the client renders a snapshot and decides nothing. ⚠ **`EngineSessions` holds one engine per character and serialises access** — the engine is *stateful*, it advances a clock and mutates itself, so two concurrent requests for one character would interleave mutations and corrupt state silently, surfacing later as a balance no ledger reconciles. ⚠ **Load → tick → act → persist is enforced at that chokepoint**, not at call sites: acting without ticking applies intent to a world frozen at the player's last visit, and not persisting discards what was just decided. ⚠ **The live-engine map is a CACHE, never the source of truth**, which is what makes eviction free — an evicted engine reloads from Postgres. ⚠ **`character_game_state.state` is `text`, NOT `jsonb`, deliberately**: jsonb would let SQL read inside a character's state, which is a *second* way to read game state able to disagree with the first, invisibly, since both look authoritative. Anything the server must answer in SQL is already a real column on `players`. ⚠ **The engine refuses, not this service** — `allocateSelfMining` returns the game's own answer, because duplicating the affordability check is how a server and a client come to different numbers.

- 2026-08-02 — **The transport's first system is REAL: compute and self-mining are authoritative over the wire** — `GameSessionService` now reads the compute ledger, the players table and the character's balance and heat, and `AllocateSelfMining` actually reserves and releases cycles. ⚠ **Allocation is ABSOLUTE, not a delta**, which is what makes it safe to retry: the client polls, and a player who taps twice on a slow link must not end up mining with double what they asked for. ⚠ **Release-then-reserve is ONE transaction, and that is load-bearing** — as two commits, a failure between them loses the player's allocation entirely and the rig goes quiet with nothing on screen saying why. ⚠ **A refusal must cost nothing**: an over-allocation is thrown rather than returned, because *returning* would commit the release and the player would lose the allocation they already had for the crime of asking for too much. ⚠ **Heat is carried as `BigDecimal`, not `int`** — caught by the compiler, then pinned by a test: heat is fractional server-side and gates detection, so rounding it in the wire type would move a player across a threshold they were deliberately sitting under. ⚠ **Mining MODE is still refused**, and the message names what does work — the chain that pays a pool is not authoritative here, and answering OK would record a choice that changes nothing while the client rendered it as a rig mining into a pool this server does not have. ⚠ **The unit tests deliberately do NOT fake the database**: the properties that matter (absolute allocation, rollback) are properties of the compute ledger's own transaction semantics, and asserting them over a fake would assert that the fake behaves like the fake — the `reconcileFootholds` failure shape, two correct halves and a defect in the join. They belong in `-Pit`.

- 2026-08-02 — **CL-8 earlier slice: the transport SHAPE exists; the engine behind it does not** — `protocol/game/{GameSnapshot,GameIntent,IntentOutcome}`, `server/session/{GameSessionController,GameSessionService}`, and the home-server prompt rewritten. ⚠ **This does not close CL-8 and must not be read as doing so.** What is built is steps 1–2 of [`../architecture/13`](../architecture/13-the-game-transport.md) §4: two endpoints (`GET /api/session/{id}/state`, `POST /api/session/{id}/intent`) instead of one per method, because `RemoteGameSession`'s last-known-good rule already requires reads to come from a local copy of authoritative state. ⚠ **`GameIntent` is SEALED as a security property, not a style choice** — the server's `switch` is exhaustive, so a new variant fails the build rather than falling through to a default that silently accepts it. ⚠ **The snapshot carries RESULTS, never the rules that produced them**: no yield curves, no thresholds, no gate tables — a client holding the rules can predict, and predicting is one step from asserting, which is the attack I14 refuses. ⚠ **Every intent currently answers REFUSED, deliberately.** Mining, the chain, breach, the filesystem and the network map are authoritative only in `solo`; answering OK would tell a player their cycles moved when nothing did, and the client would render that as their rig — a lie indistinguishable from a working feature. `GameSessionServiceTest.inventsNothing` **pins the snapshot's field list** so a mining or chain field cannot be added before the engine that fills it. ⚠ **The character in the path is not the authorisation** — that is still a seam, the same one `CharacterController` documents, and `13` §4 step 4 is where the channel supplies identity. **The home-server prompt now names the split** — sign-in and account state work, the game systems do not — instead of its previous claim that no transport existed at all; the rule it was written under survives, that a field which accepts input and then fails teaches players to distrust the address they typed.

- 2026-08-02 — **Solo character deletion asks a better question** — `MainMenuView.confirmDelete`. A confirmation already existed and said "This cannot be undone", which is true and does almost no work: it is the sentence every dialog says, so it reads as boilerplate. It now **names what is destroyed** — slot, time played, cycles — and states the part players assume is untrue: **there is no backup and no recovery**, because a solo character is one file on one machine with no account behind it and no server holding a copy. ⚠ The button says **"Delete forever"**, not OK: a destructive action behind a generic affirmative is one people press to dismiss a dialog. ⚠ **"Keep" is the default**, so Return dismisses safely — a destructive default is how a character dies to a keypress meant for the previous dialog.

- 2026-08-02 — **The secure channel is now RFC 9180 HPKE on BouncyCastle, replacing the hand-rolled Noise** — on explicit direction; `protocol/channel/HpkeChannel`, amending [`../architecture/07`](../architecture/07-transport-security.md) §4 (new §5a) and reversing `protocol`'s "no crypto dependency" note. ⚠ **T-1 is NARROWED, not closed**: the primitive is now a standard from a widely-reviewed library, so what needs a cryptographer's eye is this repo's *use* of it — key distribution, session lifetime, framing — a far smaller question than a bespoke handshake. **The old note's reasoning was sound and was overtaken by facts**, which is why it is quoted rather than deleted: it said BC "would be a 5 MB dependency for an algorithm the platform already has", and the platform turns out **not** to have two things now needed — **secp256k1** (SunEC cannot verify it; most `did:plc` accounts sign with it) and **HPKE** (SunJCE has it only on JDK 26+, and this targets 25). ⚠ **Measured 8.1 MB, not the 5 the note estimated**, into five client jars and a ~135 MB image. Chosen over `noise-java` — one maintainer, and an unmaintained crypto dependency is arguably worse than reviewed-pattern hand-rolled code because people stop looking at it — and over Tink, a third dependency for the same standard. ⚠ **Every property was verified against real BouncyCastle in a standalone probe BEFORE the class was written**, then kept as `HpkeChannelTest`: a wrong sender key means frames **do not open** (authentication with no branch to forget); **replay and reorder are rejected** by the context's sequence number rather than a nonce cache; and **the reverse direction is DERIVED** per RFC 9180 §9.8 — ⚠ reusing one context both ways would repeat sequence numbers under one key, the catastrophic AEAD failure. ⚠ **An option was surfaced and NOT taken: DID-bound mTLS via a custom `X509TrustManager`**, which would have needed no new crypto at all and would have deleted the layer for the client↔server hop — `07` §3's "TLS authenticates hostnames" objection is true of WebPKI usage, not of TLS. It remains the cheaper answer if the second layer ever looks like it is not earning its keep.

- 2026-08-02 — **CL-8's shape decided: Route A, and a snapshot/intent transport** — on explicit direction; new doc [`../architecture/13-the-game-transport.md`](../architecture/13-the-game-transport.md). **Route A** — the authoritative engine is built **server-side**, not by hosting `solo`'s engine in the server. Route B was offered and rejected: it would have been playable on a LAN far sooner, and the quarantine rule (`../architecture/12` §1) would have contained the risk of a second rules implementation, but it is not what federated play needs and the duplication would have had to be unwound. ⚠ **Transport: pinned self-signed TLS with the DID channel inside it**, per `../architecture/07` §3, which is unchanged — TLS stays mandatory. The pin must be **shown to the player on first connection and checked on every later one**; an unpinned self-signed certificate is not TLS, it is an encrypted channel to whoever answered. **T-1 applies** to the channel, and a LAN is the right place to exercise unreviewed code first — its wording is "do not let it guard a live federation". ⚠ **The client generates a transport keypair at join**, recorded against the LAN identity, which makes a LAN identity meaningfully stronger than the bearer token `12` §2 describes. ⚠ **THE DESIGN POINT: 104 `GameSession` methods must NOT become 104 endpoints.** `RemoteGameSession`'s documented last-known-good rule — every read returns the last value the server sent, even disconnected — is only implementable if reads come from a **local copy of authoritative state**, so the client is already built around a snapshot. Two endpoints: a **state snapshot** and a **sealed intent**. Every method becomes a field or a variant, so adding a system adds a field rather than a controller. ⚠ **It is also the only shape that keeps I14 checkable** — one place where a client-supplied value crosses into the rules, trivially auditable, versus "the client is never authoritative" being a property somebody re-checks per controller. ⚠ A snapshot carries **results, never the rules that produced them**: a client holding the yield curve could predict, and predicting is one step from asserting. ⚠ **Steps 2–3 of `13` §4 (the pipe, then `RemoteGameSession` reading from it) come BEFORE the engine work** — a transport with one working system proves the whole shape; an engine with no transport proves nothing a unit test had not.

- 2026-08-02 — **LAN mode: a third way to play, with a quarantine rule holding it together** — new doc [`../architecture/12-lan-mode.md`](../architecture/12-lan-mode.md), plus `server/lan/` (`ServerMode`, `LanIdentity`, `LanIdentityProvider`, `LanJoinController`, `LanAddressInterlock`, `Quarantine`) and `SessionMode.LAN`. ⚠ **Not a new idea** — `../architecture/03` §2 already said "a private/allowlisted server can ignore federation entirely and just be a single-player or friends game"; this is the unbuilt half of that sentence. **Identity is `did:easlan:<uuid>`, a synthetic DID method**, and that choice is the design: a separate `uuid` column would need a second key on every player-scoped table and a rule about which is in force, whereas a method name **carries the quarantine with the identity** — a federated server seeing `did:easlan:` knows instantly it is unresolvable, with no lookup and no flag that can go stale. ⚠ `easlan`, not `eas-lan`: a DID method is `[a-z0-9]+` in both `Did`'s pattern and the `is_did` CHECK, so a hyphen is rejected three layers below where it was constructed. ⚠ **THE QUARANTINE RULE is the load-bearing part and runs BOTH ways** — refusing to *export* LAN state is obvious; refusing to *import* federated state matters as much, because a player who could carry a real item onto a LAN server would suffer a real loss adjudicated by one machine with no accountability, and carrying it back makes the LAN server an item duplicator. ⚠ **I15 is VACUOUS in LAN mode, not violated**: it governs *cross-server* adversarial outcomes and a LAN server has none — and the quarantine is precisely what keeps that true, so "let LAN characters migrate if the operator allows it" is not a setting, it is I15 with an off switch. **I14 is untouched** — state is still the server's Postgres, and I14 says AT Proto is identity-only, not that identity must be AT Proto. ⚠ **Federation is ABSENT in LAN mode, not flag-checked** (`12` §3): a flag consulted in fifteen places is one somebody forgets in the sixteenth, so the migration controllers and `FederationConfiguration` carry `@ConditionalOnProperty(havingValue = "FEDERATED", matchIfMissing = true)` — an unset or misspelled mode gives the mode with the security machinery ON. ⚠ **`LanAddressInterlock` is the most important check in the mode**: LAN has *no authentication* — the join endpoint hands an identity to whoever asks — and that is only defensible while the network is the trust boundary, so the server **refuses to start** on a public address. The failure it prevents is completely silent: a misconfigured LAN server works perfectly, for everyone, including everyone never meant to reach it. It fails **closed** if interfaces cannot be enumerated. ⚠ **Mode is a property of the DATABASE, not a switch** — flipping LAN→federated with characters intact is "import every quarantined character at once". Opens **LAN-1…LAN-5** (allowlist default, server address changes, whether a real DID may play on LAN, mDNS discovery, and whether permanent loss is the right default between friends).

- 2026-08-02 — **The Option C loop is closed in code** — `client/oauth/ServiceAuth` mints a token from the player's PDS, `HomeServerSignIn` presents it, and the server receives it at `SignInController` (`POST /api/sign-in`) with `ServerIdentityController` (`GET /api/server`) telling the client which audience to bind to. ⚠ **Scope: `atproto rpc:com.atproto.server.getServiceAuth?aud=*`, with a documented FALLBACK** — granular scopes are still rolling out, and an older authorization server refuses the whole request rather than granting a subset, so the flow retries with identity-only and reports `canJoinHomeServer() == false` up front instead of failing at the join. ⚠ **`lxm` is pinned, only `aud` is wildcard** — the spec permits one, and this is the right way round because the method is fixed forever while the home server is not chosen until after sign-in. ⚠ **`canMintServiceAuth` reads the GRANTED scope, never the requested one.** ⚠ **A residual is recorded rather than papered over: the home server names its own audience.** A hostile server cannot *use* a token minted for someone else (`aud` is checked against the receiving server's own DID) but can induce a client to mint one for a third party and relay it there; closing it needs the DID to arrive from `08`'s signed descriptor or a discovery list (`11`), and `HomeServerSignIn.discoverDid` is a separate documented method so there is one call site to repoint. ⚠ **A 403 from a home server is reported as the ALLOWLIST specifically** — servers are closed by default (`../architecture/03` §1), so that is almost always what it is, and a generic refusal sends the player to inspect their account when the answer is "ask the operator". ⚠ **Not run against a live PDS end to end**; `../architecture/10` §7 states exactly what is outstanding.

- 2026-08-02 — **The sign-in panel was RENDERED, and it found a pre-existing bug that is not in it** — `client/ui/OnlineAccountSnapshot`, twelve images (two storage modes × six palettes). The panel lays out correctly in all of them. ⚠ **What rendering caught is that `theme.css` paints ALL prompt text in `-es-dim-3`** — the greeble/texture token that `ContrastTest` deliberately *exempts* from the 3:1 floor, because holding a hairline to a text threshold would turn every border into a stripe. Prompt text is text, so every placeholder in the client is painted in a token exempt from the contrast check precisely because it is not meant to be text; on **uOS Classic**, the only light palette and the one CLAUDE.md says catches this class of bug, the placeholder is close to invisible. Affects 18 `setPromptText` sites, not one screen. ⚠ **Deliberately not spot-fixed** — it is a global theme change interacting with a documented exemption list, and the real gap is that the exemption is by *token name* rather than by *token used as texture*. Spun out as its own task.

- 2026-08-02 — **Stage 6 done: service-auth JWT verification, with BouncyCastle** — `server/identity/ServiceAuthVerifier`, `ServiceAuthReplayGuard`, `ServiceAuthIdentityProvider`. **Closes W-6.** BouncyCastle is declared **server-only**, for the one reason that SunEC cannot verify secp256k1 and most `did:plc` accounts sign with it. ⚠ **Registering BC is NOT enough, and that is the trap inside the trap** — measured with BC registered on OpenJDK 26, `Signature.getInstance("SHA256withECDSA")` is still answered by **SunEC**, which accepts the key and refuses only at `verify()`, so the JCA never falls through. **The provider must be named explicitly.** ⚠ **The algorithm name also differs by provider**: JOSE wants raw `R‖S`, SunEC calls it `SHA256withECDSAinP1363Format`, and **BC does not have that name at all** — it is `SHA256WITHPLAIN-ECDSA`. Neither works on the other, so `MultibaseKey` now caches a *(provider, algorithm)* pair per curve, probed by a real sign-and-verify round trip rather than by asking whether an algorithm exists. ⚠ **The verifier's checks, each load-bearing:** the algorithm comes from the **key**, never the token's `alg` (algorithm confusion is inexpressible rather than guarded against); `kid` must be `#atproto` (a document may publish several keys and the holder must not choose); **`aud` must be this server's DID** — without it a token minted for one honest home server is replayable at every other server in the federation, which is the single most important claim check; bounded lifetime; and `jti` replay, consumed **after** the signature so a forged copy cannot burn a real token's id. ⚠ **Replay state is in memory and says so** — it does not survive a restart and is per-process, both acceptable for a ≤5-minute token on a single-process home server, both wrong the day it runs behind a load balancer. ⚠ **Two of the thirteen tests were weak and were caught by neutering the signature check**: one tampered with the *issuer*, so the fake directory 404'd and it passed for the wrong reason; the other ended the token with a bare `.`, which `split()` collapses to two segments so it was rejected as malformed before the signature was considered. Both rewritten so only the signature can reject them, then re-verified against the neutered build.

- 2026-08-02 — **The sign-in UI is wired, and a password field was REFUSED** — `view/OnlineAccountPanel`, `oauth/SignInFlow`, and an "Add an online account" entry on the login screen above the home-server field. ⚠ **The request was for a username/password panel; that is not buildable on top of OAuth and was not built.** OAuth exists so a third-party app never sees the password — an in-app password box would mean either `com.atproto.server.createSession` with app passwords (App-Password-equivalent breadth over the real social account, which `../architecture/02` §3 forbids) or training players to type their Bluesky password into a game, which is the exact phishing shape the flow exists to make unnecessary. ⚠ **The panel says so on screen** rather than leaving the absence to read as a missing feature, and names the rule against *itself*: "anything that asks for your Bluesky password outside your browser is not to be trusted — including a future version of this screen." Two fields: **handle** (the username) and **server** (defaulting to `bsky.social`). ⚠ **Two paths in, verified differently** — by handle the DID is known before the browser opens, so `sub` is checked against it; by server alone the account is unknown, so the check is the spec's substitute (resolve the returned `sub` and confirm its PDS binds back to the authorization server that just authenticated it). ⚠ `SignInFlow` owns the two checks that live at the seam and nowhere else: the **`state`** echo (CSRF — without it somebody can feed the loopback listener a code from *their* browser session and attach this player's rig to their account) and **`iss`** (mix-up defence). ⚠ **The client now opens a browser**, which `CLAUDE.md` recorded it had never done — Credits prints handles rather than linking them because "opening a browser would throw the player out of a full-screen game". That reasoning is about a gratuitous link; here the redirect *is* the protocol. Via JavaFX `HostServices.showDocument`, not a subprocess and not `Desktop.browse`. ⚠ **The flow blocks for up to five minutes** waiting on the player, so it runs on a virtual thread and returns to the FX thread — on the FX thread it is a frozen window that reads as a crash.

- 2026-08-02 — **Finding a home server explored; new doc [`../architecture/11-finding-a-home-server.md`](../architecture/11-finding-a-home-server.md)** — server↔server discovery is built (`08`), and the missing half is the client's **first** server. ⚠ **The finding that reframes it: `03` §1 makes home servers closed by default, so a list of servers is not useful — a discovery feature returning twenty servers the player may join none of turns "I don't know where to play" into "I tried twenty things and all rejected me", which reads as the game being broken.** The requirement is therefore "find servers that will have me", which needs a **join-policy field on the signed descriptor** before any of the mechanisms are worth showing. Options weighed: manual address (keep unconditionally), a project seed list as bootstrap (recommended, as a *hint* only — `03` §2's "low-trust index, not an authority" survives because every entry is verified against its own descriptor), peer exchange to widen (nearly free, but `08` §6 **G-3**'s Sybil question bites harder for a client than a server), and LAN mDNS (the only option needing no external service, and the one that serves the spare-box pitch — cost is a dependency the client's austerity argues against). ⚠ **Discovering servers from the player's AT Proto identity was REJECTED** on scope and coupling, not on I14: publishing to the PDS needs write scope that `02` §3 forbids, and reading the social graph would couple server choice to the informant/dossier system in `12`. Recorded because it will be proposed again. Opens **HS-1…HS-4**.

- 2026-08-02 — **The client-side OAuth flow is built, and credentials go in the OS keychain** — `client/oauth/`: `OauthDiscovery`, `Jose`, `DpopKey`, `Pkce`, `OauthClient`, `LoopbackReceiver`, `TokenStore` + keychain/encrypted-file. Closes stage 3 of [`../architecture/10`](../architecture/10-oauth-and-did-resolution.md) §7 **except the UI** — nothing calls it from a screen yet. ⚠ **Two decisions were taken on explicit direction and both reverse `10` §6's original advice.** (1) **JOSE is hand-written over JDK primitives** rather than nimbus-jose-jwt, because that would be the client's first third-party runtime dependency and the austerity is deliberate; T-1's warning is about designing a handshake, not formatting a token. ⚠ The dangerous part is named: **`SHA256withECDSAinP1363Format`, never `SHA256withECDSA`** — the latter is DER and JOSE wants raw R‖S, and a hand-rolled conversion that forgets to pad a short `r` works ~255 times in 256, ships, then rejects one login in a few hundred. (2) **Credentials go in the platform credential store**, falling back to an encrypted file. ⚠ **This is the first subprocess the client has ever spawned**, which `CLAUDE.md` recorded as a property it did not have — bounded to one class, never a shell, and **the secret goes in on STDIN, never argv**, because process arguments are world-readable on Linux and visible to `ps` everywhere. ⚠ **"Totally secure" is not on offer and the code says so**: no desktop store resists an attacker already running as the user, so `isPlatformSecured()` exists to let the interface state which mode is in force rather than imply the stronger one. ⚠ **Two macOS findings came from probing the real keychain, not from review** — `security add-generic-password -w` prompts **twice**, so a single stdin write stores nothing, prints "passwords don't match" and **still exits 0** (it fails as a success); and a missing item is a non-zero exit rather than empty output, so reading after sign-out threw where it should have reported absence. Both are why the availability check **round-trips a canary** instead of testing that a binary exists. ⚠ A third bug was caught by its own test: the encrypted-file store bound the DID as GCM **AAD**, which cannot work — AAD must be supplied to decrypt, and the DID is inside the ciphertext.

- 2026-08-02 — **W-1 closed, DNS rebinding closed, and a correction that reversed twice** — `protocol/identity/MultibaseKey`, `HttpResponseReader`, a rewritten `HardenedHttpClient`, and `server/identity/AtprotoDidPublicKeyResolver`. ⚠ **The secp256k1 story is the one to read.** It was first recorded as "ES256K needs BouncyCastle", then "corrected" to "SunEC supports it, no dependency needed", then corrected back — because the second measurement was taken on **IBM Semeru (OpenJ9)** while Maven builds on **Homebrew OpenJDK (SunEC)**, two JDK 26 builds on the same machine that disagree. The truth: **on stock OpenJDK, secp256k1 cannot be verified**, and **three API layers say otherwise first** — the curve name resolves, `KeyFactory` builds a key, and `Signature.initVerify` *succeeds*; only `verify()` fails. ⚠ **Every cheap availability probe therefore returns true on exactly the JVM where the curve does not work**, `initVerify` included, which was this code's first probe. `MultibaseKey.secp256k1Available()` calls `verify()`; `decode` refuses an unusable curve up front with the cause named, rather than returning a key that fails on the request path. ⚠ **Confirmed against a real account** — the atproto docs' own `did:plc:ewvi7nxzyoun6zhxrhs64oiz` publishes a secp256k1 key, so this is the common case, not an edge. It blocks `../architecture/10` §1 stage 6 on stock OpenJDK and affects nothing else (provenance is Ed25519, DPoP is P-256). ✅ **DNS rebinding is fixed rather than accepted:** `HardenedHttpClient` no longer uses `java.net.http`, because that API cannot express "connect to this address, speak TLS for that hostname". It resolves once, checks every address, connects a `Socket` to the address, and layers TLS with the four-argument `createSocket(Socket, host, port, autoClose)` so SNI and certificate verification use the real name against a pinned connection. ⚠ `setEndpointIdentificationAlgorithm("HTTPS")` is load-bearing — a raw `SSLSocket` validates the chain but **not** the hostname, which would make pinning worse than useless. ⚠ **The cost is a hand-written HTTP/1.1 reader**, split out as `HttpResponseReader` so the risky part is testable against a `ByteArrayInputStream`; verified live against `plc.directory` and a real bidirectional resolution of `bsky.app`. ✅ **`java.naming` measured present** — jpackage's non-modular mode links 51 JDK modules — so that residual is moot today; `TxtLookup.systemAvailable()` plus a startup warning makes it loud if anyone trims the image, which this repo already wants to do.

- 2026-08-02 — **The DID/handle resolution floor is BUILT, and it landed in `protocol`, amending that module's charter** — `protocol/identity/` (SSRF guard, hardened HTTP client, DID document + resolver, bidirectional handle resolver), `server/identity/VerifiedHandleDirectory`, and the handle refresh in `SignInService`. Closes stages 1 and 2 of [`../architecture/10`](../architecture/10-oauth-and-did-resolution.md) §7. ⚠ **The placement contradicts what `10` §6 said a few hours earlier** — "one implementation per module, so the SSRF rules are written once each", which is two SSRF denylists and therefore **one denylist that is wrong**. Both sides genuinely need this: `../architecture/04` §6.2 requires the provenance verifier to run *client-side and offline*, and that verifier's missing half (**W-1**) is exactly DID→key resolution, whose interface (`SigningKeyDirectory`) already lives in `protocol`. ⚠ **So `protocol`'s charter went from "exactly two things" to three, and the module now does network I/O, which it never did before.** That is the genuinely new thing, and `ArchitectureRulesTest` confines it to `identity` and refuses it in every other package — a wire type that phones home to fill in a field would be authoritative-state-by-the-back-door. ⚠ **The charter's own name check fired during the work**, refusing `DidDocument.Service` (a DID document's word for an endpoint) because the rule bans any type here ending in `Service`; renamed to `ServiceEndpoint` rather than carving the first exception into a deliberately blunt rule. ⚠ **`SignInService` turned out to need three handle states, not two:** "no resolver wired" and "checked, nothing verified" must not collapse into one `null`, or a server whose DNS breaks silently falls back to displaying **unverified** handles — precisely the outcome the bidirectional check exists to prevent. Hence `VerifiedHandleDirectory.canVerify()`. ⚠ **Verification ships OFF and that is not incompleteness:** there is no real identity provider yet, so verifying the handle of an identity nobody authenticated is a check that looks like security and is not; it belongs on at stage 5. ⚠ **Two residuals are recorded rather than papered over** — DNS rebinding (the guard judges an already-resolved address and the JDK client re-resolves on connect; closing it needs a pinned `InetAddress`, which the JDK does not expose) and `TxtLookup.system()`'s dependency on the `java.naming` module, whose absence from a jlink image degrades handle resolution to the HTTPS method alone **silently**. Every regression test here was **verified against the unfixed code first** — all eight fired, each naming its own defect.
