  What the grid buys is the thing a list structurally cannot show: **capacity**. `01` §6 makes storage a strict capacity/exposure trade and **I12** makes vault capacity the scarce half, but six items in a six-slot vault and six in a sixty-slot zone render identically as a list. ⚠ `STARTING_VAULT_CAPACITY` had been declared since the day storage was written and **read by nothing** — the grid is what finally made it visible. Enforcement is still absent and drawn honestly as over-capacity (`8 / 6`) rather than clamped; it is a *rules* change and belongs with the Cold Storage Expansion schematic, since a hard cap of 6 with no way to raise it is a different game from the one §6 describes. ⚠ The `est. EC to replace` figure §5.2 permits is **omitted entirely** — the client is not told an item's gate or price, and a fabricated total on the one screen whose job is to say what a raid would cost is worse than none.

- 2026-07-27 — **A Cyberdeck palette, and §9's bezel ban amended** — recorded in `ui-design-language.md` §1, §9 and the new §9.2. Two halves of one request, and only one of them needed a contract change.

  **The palette needed none.** Themes are the sanctioned place to vary colour — "every theme is the same deck with a different palette" — so `theme-cyberdeck.css` is rain-lit teal glass under a sodium accent and touches no component rule. ⚠ The accent stays **single and keeps its meaning**: the obvious cyberpunk move is hot magenta for one thing and cyan for another, which is exactly the "second accent hue, or a semantic color system" §9 rejects, so the sodium orange means what amber means on every other skin — money you have, and live work.

  **The casing needed §9 reopened.** Bezel was cut in §9 and *pointedly kept cut* in §9.1 when four other artefacts were permitted. Five styles now ship, off by default, under §9.1's identical four conditions. ⚠ The condition that shaped the implementation is that it may not cost legibility, and it is **structural rather than maintained by hand**: the frame is drawn in a **margin** and the deck is inset by exactly that margin, so no content is ever underneath it — a casing over the top strip would hide the compute readout, which is pillar **C2**. `BezelStyle.margin()` is both the inset and the drawing width, and a test fails the build on a style without one. ⚠ **Vignette stays cut and this does not reopen it**: the objection to a vignette was never about frames but that it "dims real content by position rather than by meaning", and a casing in a margin dims nothing. Corner brackets are left **open in the middle** on purpose — a frame closing on all four sides is what §9 objected to, because it puts the interface inside a picture of a device.

- 2026-07-27 — **A pool payout names its pool, and stops claiming to be a coinbase** — `ChainExplorer`, `ChainTransaction.counterpartyLabel`. The ledger's `From` column showed `0x8f3c…a219` for the row a player most needs to recognise. It now shows `THE COMMONS`, carried **beside** the address rather than replacing it, so `04` §3.1's audit still works.

  ⚠ **Finding the label found a real bug underneath it.** Solo and pooled payouts both credit `SELF_MINING`, so keying "is this minted?" off the entry *type* marked **every pooled payout as a coinbase** — rendered from the zero address, discarding the pool address the engine had carefully stamped on it, and contradicting `LedgerEntryState`'s own rule that a pool payout is not a block reward because the pool paid it out of its own balance. A minted entry has no sender and a pool payout has one; that is the test now. `MempoolTest.coinbaseHasNoSender` had been passing on a **pooled** rig, which is exactly how the bug survived. ⚠ A label is only ever attached to an address the client can **verify** — pool addresses are derived from public ids, so matching one is a fact; everything else gets none, because a name rendered where an address belongs is how a transfer from a stranger gets mistaken for a payout.

- 2026-07-27 — **Items drag between storage mounts, and the top strip wraps** — `Views.storage`, `client/ui/widgets/WrapStrip.java`, `../client/06` §5.1a.

  **Drag.** The whole mount is the drop target rather than each cell, and it highlights only for a mount the item is not already in — accepting a transfer mode gives the drop cursor, so accepting a move that will not happen tells the player it will. The drop reports the **engine's** answer to `setDropCompleted`, not "a drop happened", so a refused move (a full mount, once capacity is enforced) ends the gesture as a failure instead of animating home while the message says otherwise. ⚠ Three JavaFX traps, each of which cost a round: a `DataFormat` is a **process-wide singleton** and constructing one twice throws, so it is looked up first; a drag still delivers `MOUSE_CLICKED` to its source on release, so the click handler needs `isStillSincePress()` or every successful drag also left a stale selection; and the drop **rebuilds the panel the gesture is running on**, so the refresh is deferred with `runLater` or the source is detached before `DRAG_DONE` is delivered.

  **The top strip wraps.** It was an `HBox`, which squeezes and then clips rather than wrapping — at 200% UI scale in a 1280px window the deck is 640 logical pixels wide and most of the strip was simply gone. A `FlowPane` wraps but has no growing child, so the flex spacer that right-aligns the balance and clock (§3) would be lost at the width nearly everyone plays at. `WrapStrip` does the HBox thing while the content fits and the FlowPane thing when it does not; **at any width where the strip fitted before, the layout is what the HBox produced**. Measured: 57px tall at 1280, 113px at 980 and below. ⚠ The window controls are **pinned** out of the flow — they are the only way to minimise, maximise or close an undecorated Stage, and a control that migrates to the second row as the window narrows is the one control that must not. ⚠ Found by rendering: `setSpacer` also added the node to `getChildren()` while the caller added it positionally, which is `IllegalArgumentException: duplicate children added`.

- 2026-07-27 — **The resolution is the viewport's, and the casing became a machine** — recorded in `ui-design-language.md` §9.2. Settings → Window now sets the **screen inside the machine**, not the OS window: choosing 1920 × 1080 gives the deck 1920 × 1080 and the casing is added beyond it, so the window is `(resolution + 2 × casing) × scale`. Measured: a 1280 × 800 viewport with the 26px casing produces a 1332 × 852 window. Before this the casing was subtracted *from* the resolution, so a 20px casing turned a 1920-wide choice into an 1880-wide deck and the number in Settings described something the player never got.

  ⚠ **This decoupled UI scale from the viewport, which is a real simplification rather than a side effect.** The window used to be sized *to* the resolution with the deck laid out at `physical / scale`, so 1280 × 800 at 150% left the deck 853 logical pixels — under §3's 860 floor — and Settings had to hide size/scale combinations that did not hold. Now the deck always gets the full resolution in layout units and the scale only changes how large those pixels are drawn. `WindowSize.usableAt` and `fitsWithin` collapsed into one `fitsOnScreen`, and the only remaining constraint is whether the window fits the display.

  **The casing styles are machinery now**: `Casing` carries vent slots, corner fixings, a port block down one flank and a stamped designator plate; a new `Cable loom` dresses three cable runs at different inset lanes with right-angle bends, junction clamps and terminators. Vents and ports are the **void showing through** the band rather than marks painted on it, which is what makes them read as holes in a panel. ⚠ The trim is **asymmetric on purpose** — real equipment has a front, and identical trim on all four sides reads as a picture frame, which is what §9 objected to about bezels in the first place. ⚠ The junction clamps are the one place the casing takes the accent, and it keeps §2.1's meaning: a live connector is the only part of a shell that is actually powered.

  ⚠ **A flaky test was introduced and caught within the same session.** `MempoolTest.coinbaseHasNoSender` was switched to SOLO (correctly — it had been asserting on a *pooled* payout, which is the bug it now guards). But a solo rig at 90 cycles expects a block every ~4.3 hours against an exponential wait, so the fixed five-hour run it inherited found nothing about 30% of the time. It mines **until** a block is won now, with an early break and a 50-hour bound. It cannot be made exact: the RNG seed is derived per character, which is itself the fix that stopped every save generating an identical world.

- 2026-07-27 — **Four extreme casing styles, and §9.2's motion condition amended** — `ui-design-language.md` §9.2. `Gothic plate` (riveted plate, corner buttresses, hazard chevrons), `Terminal panel` (blinking status lamps, toggle switches, a grille), `Chrome 3.1` (raised bevel, title bar, drawn control boxes) and `Motif` (double bevel, corner grips, square buttons). Ten styles total, still off by default.

  ⚠ **§9.2's third condition was written as "nothing here moves" and that is no longer true.** `Terminal panel`'s lamps blink. The condition has been corrected to §9.1's actual rule — *what moves obeys §5* — and the lamps run on `Pulse.animate`, the decorative channel that `prefers-reduced-motion` freezes. ⚠ Frozen **lit**, not dark: a panel whose indicators all went out reads as powered off, which is a wrong statement about the machine where a still lamp is only a less lively one. The ticker is stopped in `Bezel.dispose()`, wired from `DeckShell.dispose` beside the CRT layer's.

  ⚠ **Two styles imitate window chrome, which §9 bans outright.** That ban protects §0's premise that the player never sees their own operating system — and a thirty-year-old window manager is nobody's operating system, so `Chrome 3.1` and `Motif` read as a retro machine rather than as the host showing through. Their bevels are legal on their own terms: §2.1 says depth comes from **brightness, never shadow**, and a bevel is a light edge against a dark one. No blur, no drop shadow, both still banned and machine-checked.

  ⚠ **`Gothic plate` is genre, not iconography** — rivets, plate and chevrons, deliberately none of the protected emblems the obvious reference is known for. ⚠ Control-box glyphs are **drawn as shapes, never as text**, so no character the bundled fonts might not carry can reach the casing (`GlyphCoverageTest`).

- 2026-07-27 — **There were two network windows; `map` is removed** — recorded in `../client/05-tool-windows-and-layout.md` §2.5. Reported as *"the networking tool was functional a few commits back, it seems to have been reverted"*, and the same for breaching. **Nothing had been reverted and neither tool was broken.**

  The evidence, in order. Neither commit in the window touched a single net or breach file (`git show --stat`). All nineteen tool windows built and laid out. On a fresh character a sweep discovered 5 of 5 machines and a breach opened with a snapshot and two actions. On the reporter's own save — copied, never mutated — the BASE sweep chip's exact call path returned status 0 and took `knownNodes` from **0 → 5** and `breachTargets` from **0 → 5**.

  ⚠ **The actual fault was two windows about the network, and the reachable one was inert.** `map` ("Network map", `Shortcut+2`) held a read-only table with **no sweep control**, so it was permanently empty for anyone who had not swept elsewhere — and it carried a note reading *"Breach targeting is not built"*, stale since the breach window shipped. `netmap` ("Network", `N`) was the real tool. A player pressing the digit landed on an empty table that told them the feature did not exist.

  ⚠ **Breach looked broken for the same reason, one step removed**: breach targets are swept hosts, so an unswept map means an empty target list. Two tools appearing to fail together had one cause.

  `map` is deleted and `netmap` **inherits its `Shortcut+2`**, so the habit that caused the confusion now lands on the tool that works and the digit row stays contiguous. Nothing was lost with it — `netmap` has had a LIST view on a chip beside GRAPH and FOLDERS the whole time, which is the "table you sort" the split was justified by. `WindowCatalogueTest.onlyOneNetworkTool` fails the build if a second network window ever appears.

  ⚠ **Worth keeping as a rule: `scan` is not `sweep`.** `scan` audits your own rig for parasites; `sweep` probes a network you do not own. `GameEngine` §724 says so in a comment and the log lines differ, but a player who runs `scan` and watches the map stay empty has been told nothing useful. An empty-state hint on both windows is the obvious follow-up and is **not** built.

- 2026-07-27 — **Five interface refinements, and a CSS parse error that had been silently killing half the stylesheet** — `ui-design-language.md` §2.1a.

  **(1) The deck fades up instead of flashing.** The flash was the Scene's default fill — **white** — showing for the frame between the Stage taking a new Scene and CSS resolving on it. The scaler's holder now paints `-es-void` (`.es-scene-ground`), so the colour stays in the stylesheet where §10 criterion 2 requires it, and `Motion.fadeIn` brings the deck up over 900ms. ⚠ **`Interpolator.DISCRETE`, nine steps** — a `FadeTransition` is a linear tween and `UiContractTest` fails the build on `LINEAR` outside the sweep bar. Stepping is also the truer effect: a tube coming up to brightness rises through visible levels.

  **(2) The balance counts, and the movement flashes beside it.** Green for a credit, `alarm` for a debit, on the stepped `Pulse` driver. ⚠ The first call **seeds** rather than animating — opening a 4 000 EC save would otherwise count from zero and announce four thousand ethecoin of income that never happened. ⚠ The step is recomputed from the **remaining** distance each frame so a second payout landing mid-count is absorbed; mining pays in bursts, so that is not a rare case. ⚠ Under reduced motion the value snaps and the delta is **held rather than dropped** — which way the money went is information, not decoration.

  **(3) The heat thermometer is horizontal, under its label.** A tall widget in a strip of wide short cells forced the whole strip taller for one readout. It is now label-over-meter, which is the anatomy every other cell already has. ⚠ Cold stays at the origin, so the bulb is on the **left**; a scale filling right-to-left would be read backwards by everyone.

  **(4) Network nodes carry a lock.** `[/]` breached (green), `[!]` patched — locked out (warn), `[#]` never breached. ⚠ ASCII, because §9 bans icon fonts and `GlyphCoverageTest` fails on any literal outside the bundled faces, which have no padlock. ⚠ The lock answers a **different question** from the ink-level marker beside it: the ink says how much is *known*, the lock says whether the way in is *open*. Those came apart the moment a host could be breached and then patched — such a host is richly known and shut, and overloading the ink level would have made "patched" read as "less well known". ⚠ **Nothing sets `patched` true yet** — no rule patches a host. The field, its wire type and its rendering exist so the state has one meaning the day a mechanic lands; **the mechanic itself is unproposed and needs designing.**

  **(5) The clock shows SESSION and LOCAL, with UPTIME and SERVER in a tooltip.** ⚠ UTC is labelled **SERVER** on purpose: a federated home server is authoritative for when things happened (I14) and every timestamp on the wire is UTC, so the clock a player checks a server log against has to be the same one the log is written in. "UTC" alone would be true and would not say why anyone should care.

  ⚠⚠ **The real find: a CSS syntax error silently disables every rule after it, and JavaFX only whispers it to the log.** A comment inserted mid-selector produced `Expected LBRACE at [1216,0]`, and **everything past line 1216 of `theme.css` stopped applying** — the thermometer among it, which is how it was caught (`bg=null` on nodes with correct classes and bounds). The cause was a replacement matching `.es-netmap-foothold` when the real rule was the **descendant** selector `.es-netmap .es-netmap-foothold`. Two lessons worth keeping: a stylesheet edit needs `CssParser` warnings checked, not just a green build; and a bare class selector in that block is also *weaker* than its neighbours and loses to them even when it parses.

- 2026-07-27 — **BREACH on a node clears a finished attempt instead of reopening it** — `BreachView`, `BreachArming.rearm`. Reported as the breach window showing the previous breach after an abandon or a success.

  Two faults, and they compounded. **(1)** A resolved breach deliberately stays on the save until it is dismissed, so an outcome slate survives closing the window — but `open = session.breach().isPresent()` is true for a resolved one, which hides *both* the target list and the launch panel. Arming a node from the map therefore raised the window onto the previous attempt's obituary with no control on screen but Dismiss. **(2)** `BreachArming.arm` no-ops on an unchanged id, so pressing BREACH twice on the same node was inaudible to the panel — which is why the report says "regardless if it is the same node or not".

  The fix is `rearm` for the map's control, which always notifies, plus a clear in the panel's refresh.

  ⚠ **RESOLVED ONLY, and this must stay that way.** A live attempt holds reserved compute that aborting does not refund (`05` §4), so clearing one because the player brushed a node on the map would spend their cycles for them. The check is on `resolved()`, never on `isPresent()`. Verified both ways: after an abort the slate clears on arming, and a live breach survives it.

  ⚠ It cannot loop — `dismissBreach` fires `onChange`, which re-enters the refresh, and by then `session.breach()` is empty so the branch is not taken again.

- 2026-07-27 — **Offset ciphers can arrive part-solved, and abandoning a breach costs noise** — `05` §3.1's cipher is `[PROPOSAL]`, so both are changes to a proposal rather than to settled design.

  **Part-solved boards.** A cipher board has a **55%** chance of arriving with 1–3 columns already correct, and a **12%** chance of a further 1–2 on top. ⚠ Capped at **a third of the board**, which is the part that matters: without it a 6-byte tier-1 board could arrive with 5 of its 6 columns done, which is not a shorter puzzle but an absent one. A third scales the relief with the thing it relieves, so the full give only ever lands on the long boards — 2 columns at tier 1, 5 at tier 5. Measured over 4 000 boards per tier: ~45% get nothing, and the 4–5 cases are 2% and 0.4%.

  ⚠ **Given columns are LOCKED, not merely pre-typed.** A given cell the player could overwrite is a trap dressed as a favour — a stray keystroke on a correct column is only discovered by the commit that costs a strike. Locking is also what makes the give worth more than the keystrokes it saves: a given column does not need *checking*, which is most of the tedium. It renders dimmer than the player's own answers and says "came already solved and is locked" to a screen reader (§4.4's second channel).

  ⚠ **It does not touch I7.** Proof-of-skill gates are tier-gated, never count-gated; a part-solved board is the same tier it always was. What changed is how long a layer takes, not what clearing one proves.

  ⚠ **The generator draws a constant number of values whatever it rolls.** `Rng`'s contract is that consumption must not depend on what was produced — it is why `nextInt` has no rejection loop. The obvious `if (roll < chance) { draw more }` breaks it and would desynchronise every later draw in the breach. All five decisions are drawn every time and only then read; surplus cell picks are drawn and discarded.

  **Abandonment radiates.** Aborting a live breach now leaves **30 cycles of noise for a drawn 5–20 seconds**, making the rig briefly easier to find. Until now abandoning was the *quietest* possible exit — the breach's noise simply stopped — which made "open a breach, read the board, leave if it looks ugly" a free reroll on difficulty. It stays a sanctioned outcome: nothing is taken, and the penalty is a window the player can play around.

  ⚠ **30 keeps the documented ordering intact**: above `BREACH_NOISE_CEILING` (26), so the exit is louder than the attempt was, and below `NET_SWEEP_BASE_NOISE` (35), so "the cheapest sweep is still louder than anything a breach can do" survives. ⚠ **A miner crack never spikes** — Invariant **I9**, the same reason `resolve` zeroes a crack's heat: it is the player's own rig, and a spike there would punish backing out of a fight on their own hardware, which is the tutorial breach (`04` §5.1). ⚠ The window is stored as an **instant, not a countdown**, so it settles correctly across a quit instead of waiting to be served on the next launch.

- 2026-07-29 — **UI-7's substrate: a CloudEvents event bus inside the client, and the Spring ban narrowed to allow it** — recorded in `client/pom.xml`'s enforcer block, `client/.../events/`, and the LOG window's new EVENTS tab. **The amendment.** `client/pom.xml` banned `org.springframework:*` outright as Invariant **I14** made mechanical, and the ban is now a `<includes>` allowlist of exactly six artifacts — `spring-context` and the five transitive jars it cannot resolve without (`core`, `beans`, `aop`, `expression`, `jcl`). ⚠ **The invariant is untouched and the narrowing is the point.** I14 says game state never lives in player-controlled infrastructure and the client is never authoritative; an in-process event multicaster makes no state authoritative and reaches no network. What I14 actually protects against is `spring-web`, `spring-jdbc` and the server module, and those are still refused — **negative-tested**: adding `spring-web` to the client fails the build with exit 1. No `ApplicationContext`, no component scan, no beans; `SimpleApplicationEventMulticaster` is taken directly, because a context can add a listener after refresh and offers no public way to **remove** one, and every panel in this client is created and destroyed as windows open and close.

  **The envelope is CloudEvents v1.0.2, and it is enforced rather than described.** `CloudEvent` validates in its compact constructor with the spec section cited on every rule: the four required attributes present and non-empty (§3.1.1), `specversion` pinned to `1.0`, optional attributes absent-or-non-empty rather than blank (§3.1.2), and extension names lowercase-alphanumeric, ≤20 characters, and forbidden from shadowing a core name (§4.1). ⚠ **`id` is a generated UUID and `time` is filled by the builder**, not by callers — §3.1.1 requires `source` + `id` to be unique per distinct event, and a hand-written id is the first thing to break that. ⚠ **An extension name that breaks §4.1 is rejected, not lowercased**: coercing `retryCount` to `retrycount` means the key the publisher reads back is not the key they wrote, and the mismatch surfaces at the far end of the stream instead of at the call that caused it.

  ⚠ **`time` is `Instant.now()` and NOT the session clock** — the opposite of the rule everywhere else in this codebase, and deliberately so. An event log records when the process observed something, for debugging; a stream stamped with a test clock would file a developer's afternoon under 2026 while the exception they are chasing happened at 14:02. Nothing here is a deadline, so the rule it inverts does not apply.

  **Publication is at chokepoints, so coverage cannot drift.** Successful intents publish from `LocalGameSession.changed()` and refusals from `announce()` — the two funnels every mutation and every refusal already passed through, rather than forty instrumented methods with a typo budget. ⚠ **A refused interaction publishes too**; a stream showing only successes describes a game in which nothing was ever refused, which is the more interesting half when a session has gone wrong. ⚠ **The subject is the calling method, read off the stack** with `StackWalker`, which is what makes this cost no churn at the call sites. Window events come from `DeskManager` (opened / raised / closed / focused / refused, with `raised` distinguished from `opened` because a debugging record that conflated them would show a tool opened five times when it was opened once).

  ⚠ **Background events are DIFFED across the tick, not emitted by the rules.** Tasks finishing and blocks landing happen inside `GameEngine.tick()`, one module down — and `solo` has no broker and must not gain one, since its own enforcer rule is what keeps it framework-free. So `LocalGameSession.tick()` snapshots the running-task ids and the chain height before and after and publishes the difference. ⚠ A multi-block settle is **one** event carrying the count, not one per block: a catch-up after a long absence settles hundreds and would push every other event out of a bounded log. ⚠ A quiet tick publishes nothing, or the log would hold nothing but heartbeats within the hour.

  **Everything is logged, structurally.** The recorder subscribes **in the `EventBus` constructor**, so "all events are logged" is a property of the bus rather than of whoever remembered to attach it — an event published before the LOG window has ever been opened is still there when it is. It is a bounded ring (2000) that reports what it dropped, session-only and never persisted. ⚠ **Spring propagates a listener's exception to the publisher unless an error handler is set**, and that default is wrong here: the publisher is a game rule and the subscriber is a panel, so a repaint that threw would unwind a **purchase** with the ethecoin already spent. An error handler catches it and publishes the failure as its own event — recorded rather than printed, because a packaged client has no console behind it.

  **The LOG window.** Its previous content is now the **OVERVIEW** tab, unchanged, with **EVENTS** beside it; both rendered and compared. ⚠ The snapshot builds a **fresh Scene per tab**: `Scene.snapshot` renders what the scene last laid out, so toggling `setVisible` between two synchronous snapshots produces two identical images and "verifies" a tab it never drew.

  **UI-7 is not closed by this.** The bus is the substrate the real fix needs; the half that remains is views subscribing to it instead of rebuilding on a one-second `Pulse`. Nothing was migrated off a pulse in this pass, by design — the requirement was that behaviour not change.

- 2026-08-06 — **COMS' DIRECT tab polls Bluesky, the mark springs while it does, and messages chime** — on explicit direction. **§5 and §9's rejection list are amended for the spring**; see `ui-design-language.md` §5.2 for the four conditions that fence it.

  ⚠ **Polling is `chat.bsky.convo.getLog`, which is what the endpoint is FOR.** It returns a cursor and only what changed since it. Re-running `listConvos` plus a `getMessages` per conversation every minute would spend a large multiple of the player's own allowance to discover, almost always, that nothing happened — Bluesky publishes **5,000 points an hour** and warns that clients polling every few seconds consume it. **60 s default, floored at 15 s**, and the floor is not negotiable by the slider because this is somebody else's service and the player's own budget.

  ⚠ **It covers what the player SENT as well as received.** `logCreateMessage` fires for every message in a conversation the account is in, whoever wrote it, so a reply typed on a phone appears on the next poll. A design watching only for incoming mail leaves the desktop permanently out of step with the player's own devices.

  ⚠ **The FIRST `getLog` is history, not news.** It is called once during the initial sync to establish the cursor — without that, the first poll reports the player's entire correspondence as new and chimes once per message.

  ⚠ **Only `logCreateMessage` and `logDeleteMessage` count.** Reads, reactions, mutes and the twenty-odd membership events are real log entries and none is a new message; treating any as one chimes for somebody else opening a conversation.

  ⚠ **THE SPRING IS A NARROW §5 AMENDMENT.** §5 reads "no easing curve anywhere in the product" and §9's rejection list names **spring**. `ui/widgets/SyncSpin` is one. The four conditions: **no new animation machinery** (no `Interpolator`/`Timeline`/`KeyValue`/`AnimationTimer` — a hand-authored **table of angles**, not a function, walked on `Pulse`); **one widget, one mark**; **only while a sync is in flight**; **still under Reduce motion**. The honest reading is that the table's shape is an easing curve however it is spelled — what is defensible is that it is one 20px mark that turns only while real work happens, and removing it is deleting one file.

  ⚠ **SOUND IS `javax.sound.sampled`, NOT `javafx-media`.** Media would have played the supplied MP3 directly and would also have added `libjfxmedia` natives to **all five platform uber jars** for a one-second chime. The file was converted once at authoring time (`afconvert -f WAVE -d LEI16@11025 -c 1`) and the **23 KB WAV** ships — a fifth of what one platform's media native costs. Same argument `presence/DiscordIpc` records for writing its own IPC.

  ⚠ **A LINEAR SLIDER IS NOT A LINEAR LOUDNESS.** `MASTER_GAIN` is in **decibels** over roughly −80…+6, so feeding it a percentage makes 50 mean "+50 dB" — clamped to maximum, so every setting above a few percent sounds identical and full. The conversion is `20·log10(fraction)`.

  ⚠ **The chime rides on the NOTIFICATION the player already asked for**, so a muted facility is silent too — one decision rather than two that can disagree — and `Notifications.primed` is what stops the whole backlog chiming at startup after a few days away.

- 2026-08-06 — **A starting rig is 24 cycles, and compute has a ladder: 24 → 32 → 48 → 64** — on explicit direction, and **this amends Invariant I1**. `Balance.STARTING_CYCLES` fell from 100; `Balance.COMPUTE_RUNGS` and `rules/ComputeLadder` are the ladder; `Catalogue` carries `compute-32` (ethecoin, **1200 EC**), `compute-48` and `compute-64` (schematic + rare materials, compiled).

  ⚠ **I1 read "compute is never purchasable with ethecoin"**, because otherwise mining buys mining capacity and the master scarcity becomes a compounding flywheel. **Exactly one rung** is now purchasable. **The flywheel needs a loop** — mine → buy capacity → mine faster → buy *more* capacity — and closing it takes a second purchase that does not exist. Money moves a player up **once, ever**; above 32 the ladder is schematic-gated for its whole length. That is a head start, not a compounding one.

  ⚠ **The narrowing is mechanical.** `ComputeLadderTest.onlyTheFirstRungIsForSale` and the amended `ShortcutsTest.nothingSellsCapacity` both fail the build on a second priced rung — and the second of those is the test that *was* protecting I1, amended in place rather than deleted, because the safety argument is precisely that the exception is one item wide. **I12 is untouched**: vault capacity has no exception and `ShortcutsTest` still holds all of it.

  ⚠ **THE COSTS WERE DELIBERATELY NOT RESCALED.** A Thorough Scan still costs 35 cycles, which a starting rig cannot run at all; a T3 Firewall still holds 15, nearly two thirds of one. **That is the reason the ladder is worth climbing** — what a rung buys is *which operations are possible at all*, not bigger numbers. Rescaling every cost to fit 24 would leave the player exactly as capable at every rung. `ComputeLadderTest.theTopTierIsBehindTheLadder` pins it, so a future "fix" that rescales them has to argue with a test.

  ⚠ **Capacity is DERIVED from the upgrade items held, never read from `rig.totalCycles`** — that field is a cache, reconciled on the tick. This is the `ChainState.networkHashrate` bug pre-empted: a stored copy of a derived value went stale against a re-tune and cost a real character 29% of their income forever, silently. It is also what stops a hand-edited `totalCycles` granting the whole ladder.

  ⚠ **The ladder is climbed in order** — 48 refuses without 32 — or the amendment's "money moves you up once" reasoning is false, since a player could leave the purchasable rung unbought and climb entirely on schematics.

  ⚠ **1200 EC is priced against BREACH LOOT, not mining.** At a starting rig's own ~9.6 EC/hr it is 125 hours, which nobody will do; at `design/03` §3's 45–65 EC hauls it is twenty-odd good breaches. That puts the first rung behind the puzzle rather than behind the clock — which is what keeps it from being a mining upgrade even though it is bought with mining's currency. It is ~6× `PRICE_TOP_PURCHASABLE` and exempted from `design/03` §2's bands explicitly rather than by widening them.

  ⚠ **Applying a rung plays a reboot** (`ui/RebootSequence`): an apt-shaped upgrade log, then the boot sequence. The **upgrade is not conditional on the animation** — the rules raise the ceiling on the tick — so it is safe to skip, safe under Reduce motion, and safe to miss entirely.

  ⚠ **The materials are FILL-INS** (`ComputeLadder.Rung.materials`) pending a content decision, and the Compiler's build mechanics are still **AS-1**.

  ⚠ **The re-fixture was the bulk of the work: ~31 tests across both modules.** Every fixture that allocated 40–100 cycles as a round number was silently refused against a 24-cycle rig, and the failures surfaced somewhere unrelated — a rate assertion, a task never created, a log line that never appeared, a port-scan report that read as a merging bug. `TestSaves.bare` (client) and `GameEngineTest.bare` (engine) now put a test rig at the **top of the ladder**, the same argument as their already removing the tutorial parasite: give the test the rig its subject needs, and let `ComputeLadderTest` own the ladder. ⚠ They grant the **items**, never `totalCycles`, or the next reconcile stomps it.

- 2026-08-06 — **Defences must be OWNED to be armed, and the Detection Array's ladder was split across two gates** — on explicit direction. **The gap it closes is that the gates were published and never enforced.** `09` §1 has carried a gate and a price for every defence since the design sessions, and `LocalGameSession.armIntent` checked compute and nothing else — so a brand-new character could arm a T3 firewall, a Detection Array and the Auto-Counter Daemon without holding any of them. The unlock ladder existed in the documents, in the catalogue and in the shop, and did not exist in the game; **I2** and **I3** both rest on it. A published-but-unenforced rule is worse than a missing one, because everything reads correctly and the defect is invisible to anyone who has not tried to arm something they never bought.

  **The rule for where a tier sits**, as stated: *low-level base tools and low-level upgrades are purchasable and cost more than a consumable; high-level and rare items need a schematic to compile.* Applied:

  | Item | Gate | Price |
  |---|---|---|
  | Firewall T1 / T2 / T3 | Ethecoin | 40 / 110 / 200 EC |
  | Canary Token | Ethecoin | 8 EC |
  | Tarpit | Ethecoin | 70 EC |
  | Detection Array T1 / T2 | **Ethecoin (moved)** | 50 / 140 EC |
  | Detection Array T3 | Schematic | — |
  | Honeypot Stash | Reputation | — |
  | Auto-Counter Daemon | Schematic | — |

  ⚠ **What keeps I2 intact is that each ladder's TOP RUNG is out of the market's reach.** Money reaches the highest rung below the ceiling and never the ceiling itself — the same "top purchasable" shape `03` §2 already gives the firewall, whose whole ladder is ethecoin-gated on `09` §2's argument that it is horizontal protection limited by standing compute rather than by price. Pricing Detection Array T3 collapses that **silently**: the shop renders, the purchase works, and ethecoin has bought a permanent capability. `CatalogueTest.theTopOfEveryDefenceLadderIsNotForSale` fails the build on it.

  ⚠ **I3 is untouched.** Three tiers are three *items*, each behind exactly one gate — not one item behind two. The ownership check is per **tier**, so owning T1 arms nothing above it (`DefenceGateTest.aRungIsNotTheLadder`); a check on the *kind* would have made the cheapest rung a key to the whole ladder including its schematic-gated top.

  ⚠ **A new character is ISSUED a Firewall T1** (`Catalogue.STARTING_DEFENCE`), into the **vault** rather than the high-risk zone. Granted rather than exempted: arming requires owning with no special case, because a rule with one exception acquires a second. Without it a new player's first visit to the FIREWALL panel is ten refusals, and the reasonable conclusion is that the tool is broken rather than that the ladder is locked. It is an ordinary ethecoin-gated item — sellable and re-buyable, as `02` §2.1 requires of everything on that gate.

  ⚠ **The refusal names the gate, not the absence.** "You do not have that" is true and useless; `02` §1's premise is that a gate is legible, so "it is sold in the market", "it is compiled from a schematic and is never sold" and "it takes standing with a faction, not money" are three different sentences. The FIREWALL panel says the same thing **before** the click, in the row's ACTION cell.

  ⚠ **Two established assertions broke and both were the design colliding with an assumption that only held while these items did not exist.** `FirmwareTest.firmwareIsExpensive` asserted firmware was dearer than every other offering — Firewall T3 is 200 EC against firmware's 180, and both figures are pinned in the documents. Rewritten to the claim that was actually meant: dearer than every *consumable*, and gated behind a schematic no money buys, which is why its **total** cost is still the highest in the game. `UpgradeVersionTest.answersBeforeTheTransfer` hard-coded `NEW`; the starting firewall made a foreign firewall package correctly read `UPGRADE`, so the expectation is derived from what the rig holds now.

  ⚠ **Open — where reputation-gated stock is SEEN.** `09` §2a `[PROPOSAL]`: a heat-state-gated vendor (`02` §2.5 — "black-market brokers are only reachable while hot"), surfaced as a **TOR Marknet** tab unlocked by a module that arrives in the COMS inbox. `Balance.BLACK_MARKET_MIN_REPUTATION` / `BLACK_MARKET_MIN_HEAT` exist; **the inbox does not**, so nothing consumes them yet. See CL-9.

- 2026-08-07 — **NPC machines and operators are named, and the name became a port-scan finding** — on explicit direction. `engine/net/NpcNames` holds the three pools; `PortScanTarget.IDENTITY` is a new **eighth rung at the bottom** of the port-scan ladder; `NodeReportState.{hostName,operatorName}` store it; the network map draws it. **This changes what a sweep gives you.**

  - **Machine names are Docker's scheme** — `adjective-pioneer`, after `docker/pkg/namesgenerator`, with the pioneer drawn from computing, mathematics, physics, quantum mechanics and astronomy. It reads as a real fleet naming convention because it is one, and a player meets the same convention the first time they run a container. **Operator accounts** come from a separate pool of ordinary given names — English, Nordic, Romance, Korean, Japanese, Norwegian, Chinese and Russian — because a surveillance state's network is staffed by whoever lives there.
  - ⚠ **NO ADJECTIVE MAY BE DEMEANING.** These are real people and a good deal of the pool is living, so pairing a real surname with an insult is a claim about a person the game has no business making. Docker learned this in public: its generator special-cases `boring_wozniak` out, with the comment that Steve Wozniak is not boring — a rule over the whole list is the version of that fix which cannot be defeated by adding one more word. ⚠ The rule is **"not demeaning", not "complimentary"**: the pool is deliberately moody and a little suggestive, which is atmosphere rather than a judgement of the person.
  - ⚠ **Derived from the address, never drawn.** `TopologyGenerator`'s draw count is a pure function of the world's shape, so a draw per machine would re-roll every existing world. Hashed instead, exactly as `DocumentPool` already does — and with the same **FNV-1a**, because `String.hashCode` on addresses differing in one octet walks the pool in lockstep with the host index. That was **measured, not assumed**: the eight-name array this replaced produced `wren dana kai morgan riley sasha toma ves` — the pool in declaration order — on every server. Second time that trap has bitten, so the hash now lives in one place.
  - ⚠ **A machine name must not encode what the machine is** — naming a node's type is the 15 EC Passive Sniffer's published function (`07` §1). The scheme this replaced leaked it quietly: labels were `<server>-<index>` and host index 0 is the gateway on every server, so a trailing `-00` was a free and completely reliable "this is the gateway". An adjective and a surname correlate with nothing.
  - ⚠ **The name is no longer free at discovery, and that is the mechanical change.** A sweep used to copy the label straight off ground truth. A sweep's product is existence and adjacency; the name is now `IDENTITY`'s, or it comes from having breached the host, where the name and the account are simply in the prompt.
  - ⚠ **Adding a rung at the bottom did NOT re-price the seven above it.** Every cost was `f(depth)`, so inserting one below shifted all seven up a number and would have raised their price, duration, noise and detection risk as a side effect — invisibly, since every screen still renders. The formulas are keyed on `PortScanTarget.steps()` now and `IdentityFindingTest.theCalibratedRungsAreUnchanged` pins the seven figures literally. The new rung is floored so it is cheap without being free; a free rung is one nobody decides about.
  - ⚠ **The stored identity is WRITE-ONCE, unlike every other finding on the file.** A firewall tier is a measurement and a rescan should refresh it; a name is an identity. Since names are *derived*, editing a pool shifts every derived name at once — pinning at first contact is what makes "the operator you found when you first broke in" a fact about that break-in rather than about the current build.
  - ⚠ **Existing characters are relabelled on load** (`TopologyGenerator.relabelLegacy`), which is the **one migration in this repo** and an explicit exception to `CLAUDE.md`'s no-legacy-machinery rule. `generate` returns early when a topology exists, so without it a character made before this date would carry `home-relay-00` names forever and the only remedy would be deleting the character. Justified because **a name has no mechanical consequence** — rewriting one cannot change an outcome. Idempotent by construction rather than by a flag; the rig is skipped on `SELF`; a name already pinned on a recon file is corrected in the same pass, since write-once would otherwise defend the old one against every future scan. **Delete it the moment a build ships.**
  - **Consequences elsewhere:** `breachProtocolShare` moves in eighths rather than sevenths (`16` §5.1 — derived from `values().length`, so nothing was re-tuned); `UiTokens.NET_NODE_LINES` went 4 → 5 for the name line, with the slot reserved whether or not a name is known so the map does not re-flow when a scan lands.

- 2026-08-06 — **The offline half-weight was extended from solo to every mining mode** — on explicit direction — and the constant renamed `OFFLINE_SOLO_WIN_WEIGHT` → **`Balance.OFFLINE_MINING_WIN_WEIGHT`** to say so. **The gap it closes.** The 2026-07-29 decision below exempted pooled mining, on the grounds that a pool's hashrate is the pool's. That is still true of the *pool*; what it did not justify was exempting the *player's* pooled income, which is what the exemption actually did — a pooled character collected four hours at full rate for closing the client while a solo one collected four at half, and the default pool is pooled. Play is now strictly better per hour inside the buffered window in all three modes.

  ⚠ **One constant, and the rename is the point.** The quantity is "what this rig's hashrate is worth while nobody is at the keyboard", which is the same question in every mode. Two constants would be two figures to re-tune and one to forget, which is how solo and pooled offline income came to differ by a factor of two in the first place.

  ⚠ **Three modes, three places, because the player's hashrate enters three ways.** **Solo** — `ChainRules.drawWinner` scales the player's own share of the draw; it has to be the draw, since a solo block pays the whole subsidy plus its fees and there is no cut to scale. **PPLNS** — `MiningRules.runSelfMining` scales the player's cut of each block carrying `Won.offline()`. **PPS** — the same method scales the share clock's accrual, because a share pool pays per accepted share out of its own balance whether or not anybody found a block.

  ⚠ **Halving the chosen pool's `networkShare()` in the draw was the request as put, and was rejected.** A pool does not lose half its hashrate because one member logged off, so it would hand the freed probability to the unpooled population for four hours and leave the block explorer reporting that this player's pool underperforms during their absences. Worse, it would halve the pool blocks written to the CONTRIBUTOR record under **PPS** while reducing PPS income by *exactly nothing*, since those rows credit zero by construction — and PPS is what the default pool and every new character use. Scaling the player's own share of the proceeds costs the chain nothing and reaches every scheme. `ChainSyncTest.OfflineWeight.poolsAreUntouched` holds the chain to the same shape either way; `MiningChainTest.OfflinePoolWeight` covers the payout half, and both of its regression assertions were verified against the unweighted build first.

  ⚠ **The PPS lever is the ACCRUAL, never the payout or the target.** A share that paid half would make a share mean two things; a bigger target would re-rate the draw and stop a stored seed being a replay. Slowing the clock leaves a share worth exactly a share, earns fewer of them, and carries the residue into the next tick as it always did.

  ⚠ **The window did not move.** `OFFLINE_MINING_HOURS` stays at 4 for pooled and solo alike — pooled offline mining was *already* capped there (`ChainRules.sync` gates pool-block credit on `competing` and PPS accrues off `Sync.minedFor()`), so raising it to 8 would have been a doubling rather than the intended cut. Considered and declined in the same session.

  ⚠ **No invariant moves.** **I4** — self-mining is still zero-heat, undetectable and unseizable. **I5** — offline income stays capped and non-proportional, and this only lowers the cap's value. **I2** — nothing here is purchasable. **I6** — deployed miners are out of scope; they spend the host's compute and settle out of their own buffer.

- 2026-07-29 — **Offline solo mining competes at half weight** — on explicit direction — recorded in `Balance.OFFLINE_SOLO_WIN_WEIGHT` (renamed 2026-08-06, see above), applied in `ChainRules.drawWinner`, and published in `04-mining.md` §3.3. **The gap it closes.** `OFFLINE_MINING_HOURS` caps how *long* an absent rig keeps hashing, which is what stops a longer absence being worth more; it says nothing about an hour away versus an hour played. Four hours at full rate for closing the client is a thing to collect rather than a courtesy, so the second lever caps how *well* the rig does inside the window. Play is now strictly better per hour **inside** the buffered window as well as past it.

  ⚠ **Self-mining only, and fills only.** The live tick is untouched — a player who leaves the client running is playing, and this is not an idle-time penalty. Pools are untouched: a pool's hashrate is the pool's and competes whether or not one member's client is open, so scaling it during a fill would be this rig reaching into somebody else's rate — and would quietly make pooling the better way to be absent, which is a balance decision nobody took. The probability the player gives up lands in the **unpooled remainder**, because a block this rig did not win was still won by somebody; every pool keeps its exact `networkShare` either way.

  ⚠ **No invariant moves.** **I4** — self-mining is still zero-heat, undetectable and unseizable; a smaller number is not a risk. **I5** — offline income stays capped and non-proportional, and this only lowers the cap's value. **I2** — nothing here is purchasable, so no ceiling moved.

  ⚠ **It must not change RNG consumption**, and does not: the draw is one `nextDouble` per block whatever the mode, and the weight scales the **threshold** it is compared against rather than the number of draws. A generator whose consumption varied with the mode would stop a stored seed being a replay — `Rng`'s stated contract, and the same reason `drawWinner` rolls even for blocks the rig is not contesting. `ChainSyncTest.OfflineWeight` pins it deterministically: with the same seed, every block won offline is also won online, so a subset failure means the streams diverged.

  ⚠ **Deliberately invisible, per the request.** No readout names it. The SYNCHRONIZING screen reports what the chain did; a player comparing blocks-won against hashrate share across two sessions is doing arithmetic on a Poisson process with n ≈ 2, and a figure on screen would invite exactly that.

  ⚠ **The fixture bug this exposed is worth keeping.** Comparing an online run against a fill needs both to start from the *same* save, not from two saves built the same way — a freshly opened game draws its own initial `networkWorkTarget` seeded from the character id, so two identically-constructed rigs are already a fraction of a block apart and diverge within the hour. That looked exactly like a broken RNG contract. The fixture persists one save and loads it twice.

- 2026-07-29 — **Which breach puzzle you draw is earned, not flipped for** — on explicit direction — recorded in `16-breach-implementation.md` §5.1, `Balance.BREACH_PROTOCOL_SHARE` / `BREACH_PROTOCOL_SHARE_INFORMED` / `breachProtocolShare`, `NodeReports.known` and `BoardFactory.build`. The class was an even coin flip; it is now weighted by how complete the target's **port-scan report** is. **The offset cipher is the default** — it is the puzzle that needs nothing from the far side, since deriving an offset from ciphertext is what you do with no other handle — and **Breach Protocol is the puzzle of someone who knows the host**, its grid being that machine's own protocol surface. Zero findings draws the cipher every time; a complete report draws the grid ~95%; linear in between at one seventh per finding, so there is no threshold to discover and a player who scans one more thing sees the odds move.

  **This gives RECON its first mechanical consequence.** A report was previously intelligence a player read and acted on by hand — `07`'s tools sat beside `05`'s puzzle rather than feeding it. Now the report changes what the breach *is*.

  ⚠ **It buys a DIFFERENT puzzle, never an easier one**, and that is the load-bearing constraint. Tier, attention budget, strike limit, layer count and cycle cost are identical either way; `BreachPuzzleWeightingTest.Pricing` asserts it. **If the two puzzles ever stop being comparable in difficulty this silently becomes a discount**, and a proof-of-skill gate that can be bought down is not proof of skill (**I7**) — so it is the first thing to re-check when either is re-tuned.

  ⚠ **Not a certainty at full knowledge.** 0.95, not 1.0: a machine that can still surprise a well-prepared operator once in twenty is the fiction working, and a guaranteed puzzle means the cipher stops being practised by anyone who scans — which is `16` §5's original failure, a player drilling half the skill the gate certifies. The class is announced before anything is spent, so the residual is a surprise they can walk away from.

  ⚠ **Staleness does not count against a report** — a week-old finding still counts as known. The per-finding age is already on screen, so a player can see what has gone cold; silently discounting it would move the odds under them with nothing changing on screen.

  ⚠ **The roll is taken unconditionally, including at a weight of zero.** Skipping it for an unscanned machine would make every later draw in the breach depend on whether the player had scanned the target, so the same seed would produce different boards — `16` §2's replay rule applied to a new input. Tested on a seed where the informed attempt lands in the 5% residual, which is the only case where the two streams are comparable at all.

  **Unrelated flake fixed in the same pass.** `GameEngineTest.nothingIsWonPastTheCap` bounded `competedBlocks` at `ceil(4h / 14min) + 8` = 26. How many blocks land inside a *fixed* four-hour window is itself Poisson — mean 17.1, σ ≈ 4.1 — so that ceiling sat **2.1σ** above the mean and failed about one run in fifty on a fixture whose seed is fresh every run. It was measuring the Poisson tail rather than the cap. Now a 4σ bound with the basis stated; the cap itself is carried by the uncontested-block assertion beside it.

- 2026-07-29 — **BUG: a breached machine never became a foothold — `reconcileFootholds` had no caller** — reported from play ("I breached a node and the lock indicator was never updated"), fixed in `GameEngine.settleBreachOutcomes`, called from `resume()` and from `breachAction`. **What was wrong.** `NetRules.reconcileFootholds` is what turns a `BREACHED` resolution into a foothold and pays the host's one-time loot. It was written, documented with two ⚠ notes, and covered by five tests — and **every caller was a test**. Nothing in the shipping game invoked it. So a player could clear every layer, be told the attempt succeeded, and find the machine still reading `contact` on the map, still refusing `connect`, and still holding its loot.

  ⚠ **The failure shape is worth naming, because the code was not wrong anywhere.** Both pieces were correct and both suites were green; the defect lived entirely in the join between them, which is the one place a unit test cannot look. `NetRulesTest` even carried a comment asserting the caller existed — *"reconcileFootholds is called on every resume and after every breach"* — which was true of the design and false of the build, and reading it made the wiring look verified. **A comment describing a caller is not evidence of one.** The new `FootholdAfterBreachTest` is deliberately written against `GameEngine` rather than `NetRules`, one level up from the unit, because that is the lowest level at which the bug is visible at all. Verified by neutering the fix: all three fail, then pass.

  ⚠ **Settled on the LOAD path too, not only after a breach.** A save written before the fix carries `BREACHED` resolutions and no footholds, so without `resume()` the bug would be permanent for anyone who had already taken a machine — they would have to breach it again, on a target the game was still refusing `connect` to. This is safe because `reconcileFootholds` is idempotent *by construction* rather than by bookkeeping: `foothold` and `looted` are one-way flags on the host, so there is no settled marker to get out of step, and the loot is paid once across any number of replays.

  **The client was not at fault.** `LocalGameSession.translate` already routes a successful breach move through `changed()`, and `NetMapView` repaints on `onChange` — the map was faithfully drawing state that had never been updated.

  ⚠ **Noticed in passing: map visibility keys on `knownNodes`, port scanning keys on `host.discovered`.** Two different notions of "found", and a fixture that set only the flag produced a host the map had never heard of. They agree today because a sweep sets both, but nothing enforces that. Not fixed here; worth a look before a third caller picks whichever it finds first.

- 2026-07-30 — **Upgrades carry a version, and Get Info tells you what a foreign package is before you take it** — on explicit direction, with the version's semantics chosen by the user from three options — recorded in `protocol/game/UpgradeVersion`, `protocol/game/UpgradeOffer`, `engine/rules/Versions`, `Balance.UPGRADE_VERSION_RESALE_PERCENT_PER_MAJOR` / `MARKET_UPGRADE_VERSION_MAJOR`, `GameEngine.upgradeAt`, and the compare block in `FileManagerView`.

  **The problem.** A stolen upgrade was an opaque {@code .pkg}: 40–320 MB, no way to learn what it was without spending the transfer and the exposure to take it. Every copy of every tool was therefore the same proposition — a player who already owned the Wide Sweep had no reason to look at another one, and no reason to prefer a hard target over an easy one.

  ⚠ **A newer build is worth more and supersedes an older one. It is NOT a better tool.** This was the decision, and the option that would have made a newer version a genuinely better tool was **rejected on invariant grounds**: a capability that rises with the hardness of the machine you take it off is a ceiling reachable by grinding with no gate on it — **I2** arriving from an unexpected direction — and the item would then sit behind its catalogue gate *and* a raiding ladder, which is **I3**. The only mechanical consequence a version has is **resale value and supersession**. `UpgradeVersionTest.Capability` is the guard and is the test to keep if any other is dropped: it walks the whole catalogue asserting that no capability figure varies with the build, that resale never reaches retail at *any* version, and that a version never makes a gated tool sellable.

  **The loop.** The major number tracks the **host's tier**, so a tier-5 estate carries newer software than a tier-1 desktop — true of real estates, and the incentive to raid harder targets. The market ships `MARKET_UPGRADE_VERSION_MAJOR` = 3, deliberately the **middle** of the ladder: selling the newest would make raiding pointless, selling the oldest would make the catalogue a trap. Deterministic from item and host, never drawn — same rule `VirtualFs.upgradeBytes` already follows, and what makes "is theirs newer than mine?" answerable across visits.

  ⚠ **A version is two integers, not a string.** The whole feature is comparison, and lexically `"v1.10"` sorts before `"v1.9"` — the one question the type exists to answer would get a wrong answer that looks right. ⚠ **Parsed tolerantly**: it is a save field, so anything unreadable is `UNKNOWN` rather than an exception. ⚠ **Recorded at arrival, not derived on read** — a host's tier can change, and a package that re-derived its version would silently change build while sitting in Downloads.

  ⚠ **An item installed before versions existed is treated as OLDER, not SAME.** Claiming "you already have this build" about a build nobody knows the number of is a claim the game cannot support; the harmless reading lets the player replace it.

  **Two things the render caught**, neither visible in review: the emphasis was inverted — the *machine's* build drew dimmer than the player's, so the block read as being about their own vault — and the two version cells sat wherever their contents ended, so `none` and `v4.0` started at different x and the pair stopped reading as a comparison. Emphasis is now fixed rather than varying with the verdict, which also makes §4.4 compliance structural: nothing is encoded in contrast, because the verdict says it in words.

  ⚠ **Pre-existing content gap made legible, not filled.** Some app bundles (`Breach.app`, `Mining.app`) advertise upgrades for tools the solo catalogue does not carry. Those packages were already duds — `install` refuses them as "not an installable upgrade" **after** the player has paid for the transfer. Get Info now says so beforehand. Filling the gap means inventing catalogue items, which is a content decision and does not belong in code (`CLAUDE.md`).

- 2026-07-30 — **Firmware upgrades as a class, with the Firmware Implant as the first one** — on explicit direction — recorded in `11-rig-infrastructure.md` §3 (the source of truth), `protocol/game/UpgradeKind`, `Catalogue.Offering.kind/requiresSchematic/stopsTool`, `Repac.Refusal.NO_SCHEMATIC` / `TOOL_RUNNING`, and `Balance.FIRMWARE_IMPLANT_IMAGE_PRICE`.

  **Three rules, all of them the real behaviour of firmware.** It needs the **schematic** (authorisation) *and* a **software component** — the image — bought from the market or taken off a machine by breaching. It **costs more** than any ordinary upgrade (180 EC, above every other single purchase). And **the affected tool must be stopped**, because firmware sits underneath the program using it; a half-written firmware is how a device is bricked, and real flashing tools refuse for exactly this reason.

  ⚠ **Not a second gate — I3 is intact, and this lands on an existing precedent rather than bending one.** `02` §1.1 already sanctions the split: *"Rainbow Table is EC + schematic (buy the table, but the capability to use it is found)"*, under the standing condition that the **ceiling component is on the non-EC side**. Here the schematic is the ceiling and the image is inert without it, so `11` §4 rule 1's "no EC path, no exceptions" holds. ⚠ **§4 rule 2 checked:** it touches mining income and adds no *cycles*, so no compute-buys-compute loop (**I1**).

  ⚠ **The two conditions are checked in the order the player can act on them** — schematic first, then the running tool. A player missing both who is told to stop mining loses their hashrate and then hits a schematic refusal they were never going to clear on the way back.

  ⚠ **Deployed miners count as "the tool running", and that is the half a player will not think of.** A deployed miner spends the *host's* compute (**I6**), so nothing on the player's own rig looks busy — but it is this rig's mining software driving it, and flashing is the same interrupted write. The refusal names the count rather than saying "the tool is running", which would send them to the wrong readout.

  ⚠ **It is a refusal, never an offer to stop mining automatically.** Stopping it on the player's behalf costs them income they did not agree to lose.

  **Discoverable before anything is spent.** A firmware package is named `<tool>-firmware.pkg` rather than `-upgrade.pkg`, so `ls` shows the class; Get Info and `stat` state both conditions before the transfer. That matters more here than for software: a package that cost a download to discover you *cannot* flash would be the exact defect the Get Info work removed, wearing a new face.

  ⚠ **Firmware with no schematic named is rejected in `Offering`'s compact constructor.** That single omission is what would turn firmware into a permanent capability reachable with money alone, and it is precisely the edit somebody adding a second firmware item would make by accident. Enforced rather than documented.

  ⚠ **The image must stay expensive enough that stealing one is worth the breach.** `01` §6 makes raiding a first-class acquisition route and this design leans on it; a cheap image makes the breach route dead content and reduces the whole thing to an expensive purchase. That is the floor on the price, not the ceiling.

- 2026-07-30 — **Firmware flashes rather than installs: `.frm`, a 90-second task, and a full-panel overlay** — on explicit direction — recorded in `11-rig-infrastructure.md` §3, `Repac.FIRMWARE_SUFFIX` / `FLASH_KIND` / `beginFlash` / `completeFlash` / `frozenTool`, `Balance.FIRMWARE_FLASH_SECONDS`, and `PackageView`'s flashing overlay.

  ⚠ **`.pkg → .frm` for firmware; the `.pkg` stage is unchanged.** The `.pkg` rename **is** the confirmation lock (`04` §1.3e), derived from the ledger row on every read. Naming firmware `.frm` at both ends leaves it with no rename to make, and a bought image would become flashable before its payment was mined. `.frm` buys what `.upg` bought: the class is visible in `ls`, the file manager and the shell without any of them knowing what firmware is.

  ⚠ **The freeze is the second half of "stop the tool first".** Stopping mining at the door and permitting a restart two seconds later makes the rule ceremony; the flash's real cost is the income given up while the tool is down. Raising the allocation is refused for the duration — **setting it to zero never is**, because trapping a player's cycles inside a tool they cannot use is a bug wearing a rule's clothes.

  ⚠ **A task, so it survives a quit**, settled by `tick()` *and* `resume()` — the second because `resume()` sets `lastTick = now` and the first tick back sees zero elapsed. **The image is consumed on completion, not at the start**, so an interrupted flash costs nothing rather than costing everything.

  ⚠ **90s is not derived from the image's size**, unlike a transfer. A download is bounded by the far end's uplink; a flash is bounded by the device writing its own memory, and a bigger image does not make a slower flash on real hardware.

  **The overlay.** Full-panel and opaque over a `StackPane`, so the panel underneath is never torn down and the flash simply ends. ⚠ The warning mark is a **drawn Polygon with two Regions composing its counter-shape**, not a glyph: `U+26A0` is in neither bundled face and `GlyphCoverageTest` has already rejected it once, in `PortScanView`. ⚠ Driven by `Pulse.every` — **data, not animation** — so `UiContractTest`'s §5.1 rationing is untouched and Reduce motion keeps it, because a player who cannot see the flash progressing cannot distinguish it from a frozen client. ⚠ No radius anywhere in the block: §9's ban is unamended for anything a value is read off, and a soft-ended fill reads as a shorter fill.

  **Two defects the render caught, neither visible in review.** The fill was sized from `track.getWidth()` on each pulse, and that is **0 until the first layout pass** — so the bar was empty on the frame the panel opened and empty in every render, which is a progress bar that does not show progress. It is now a binding against `track.widthProperty()`, which is also the only version that survives a resize. And the overlay named neither the package nor the tool, so a player with two packages open could not tell which one they had committed to.

  **One unrelated wart fixed in passing:** `LocalGameSession.tasks()` gave every running task the caption *"signal strength, not certainty"* — a scan's caption, hardcoded. Harmless while scans were the only kind, and visibly wrong the moment a flash appeared under it.

- 2026-07-30 — **Files can be deleted from your own rig** — recorded in `Repac.delete`, `GameEngine.delete`, `GameSession.delete`, the node shell's `rm`, and the file manager's Delete entry.

  **Why it had to exist.** Downloads accumulated and nothing removed them. A player who has raided a dozen machines ends up with a dozen packages they will never install and — for the schematic-gated ones — cannot sell either (**I2**). A filesystem you can only add to is not a filesystem.

  ⚠ **Only files the rig actually STORES.** `VirtualFs` derives the system tree, application bundles and vault views from state and stores none of it, deliberately — a stored tree is a cache of game state that eventually disagrees with it. There is nothing to delete, so the refusal says so rather than succeeding and leaving the entry on screen.

  ⚠ **Own rig only.** `engine/rules/AccessLog` already holds the line that a remote actor **blanks** a log line rather than removing it, because a deleted row turns a legible crime into a missing file. A remote delete would quietly grant the thing that rule exists to refuse.

  ⚠ **An image being flashed cannot be deleted.** `completeFlash` drops the task silently when the image is missing, so without the guard a player could delete mid-write, wait out the minute, and receive nothing — with the log claiming a flash had run.

  ⚠ **The GUI confirms and the shell's `rm` does not, deliberately.** A real `rm` does not ask — that is what `rm -i` is for — and a terminal that behaved otherwise would teach something false about a command this client's manual documents. A right-click and a menu item is two cheap gestures and the thing at the end can be worth 180 EC. The dialog names the **resale value**, because "delete this file?" and "burn 108 EC?" are different questions. The log names it too on the way out: an item that vanishes with no trace is indistinguishable from a bug.

  ⚠ **`rm -rf /` is safe, and now has a test.** `NodeCommandsTest.unknownVerb` had used it as its canonical *unknown* verb, so adding `rm` broke it — the test catching that is the test working. `-rf` is not a flag this shell knows, so it swallows the operand and nothing is named (real `rm`'s "missing operand"); naming the root directly gets "Is a directory". ⚠ The root resolves to **no entry at all** — `entry()` finds a path by listing its parent and `/` has none — so that branch is explicit, or `rm /` would report ENOENT about the one directory that certainly exists. **There is no recursive delete and there must not be:** the filesystem is generated from game state, so there is no tree to walk.

  **Two defects fixed in passing.** `isPackage` had never learned `.frm`, so a firmware image on the rig showed no Install/Inspect/Sell — hiding the panel from the one file class where Inspect matters most. And `PackageView.create(null, …)` — the call the snapshot mains use to render package states without a save — was broken by the flashing overlay asking a null session for its task list.

- 2026-07-30 — **The package panel scrolls and wraps instead of setting its own width** — recorded in `PackageView.frame`.

  The panel pinned `setMinWidth(640)` and let its content decide the rest, so the two **SHA digests** — 71 unbreakable monospace characters each — set the width of the entire window and every other line stretched to match. It is now capped at 560 with `setFitToWidth(true)`, which inverts the relationship: the frame decides the width and the content wraps into it.

  ⚠ **`setFitToWidth` is the load-bearing half.** Without it a `ScrollPane` hands its content the content's *preferred* width, which for an unwrapped digest is the full 71 characters — so no wrapping happens and a horizontal scrollbar appears instead. Same trap as `Views.scrollable`'s `fillHeight`, from the other side.

  ⚠ **The digests are wrapped, not shortened.** `PackageManifest.shorten` exists and is deliberately unused here: the panel's whole argument is that you are shown both figures and can compare them yourself, and an elided middle is exactly where a substituted payload would hide. Both wrap at the same width, so they stay line-for-line comparable.

  ⚠ **The actions are PINNED below the scroll, never inside it.** A package with a long summary and a long refusal pushes them past the fold, and a panel whose Install button must be scrolled to reads as a panel with no Install button. ⚠ An unstyled `ScrollPane` paints Modena's **white** viewport over a dark theme — both the pane and its `.viewport` need the transparent fill.

- 2026-07-30 — **`Ethecoin` and `Cycles` render as themselves; the "no display formatting" note is REVERSED** — recorded in `protocol/game/Ethecoin#toString`, `protocol/game/Cycles#toString`, and `ValueTypeRenderingTest`.

  **The bug the player saw:** a delete confirmation reading *"This would sell for Ethecoin[minorUnits=480] on the secondary market."*

  **The note that caused it.** `Ethecoin` carried a deliberate decision: no display formatting, because `"25.00 EC"` is a localization choice (separator, symbol placement, abbreviation) belonging to the client, and the record's generated `toString` is unambiguous, which is what logs and test failures need. Both halves are true and the conclusion was still wrong — it ignored what a generated `toString` **is**: the thing you get by accident. `"you have " + balance` compiles, renders without complaint, and prints the internal at the player.

  ⚠ **Five player-facing surfaces had it before anyone noticed** — a delete confirmation, a storage log line, `inv`'s balance row, a balance readout, and a refusal telling the player what they could afford — and `Views.spec` had already grown a local comment warning the next person. **A footgun that has fired five times and acquired a folk warning is a defect in the type, not in the five call sites.** The correct formatter existed as **eleven private copies** of `money(long)` across both modules, which is exactly why nobody reached for it.

  **The localization argument survives untouched.** A *localized* amount — grouped separators, symbol in the reader's position, large amounts abbreviated — is a different method and still belongs to the client. What was missing was a safe **canonical** default for the concatenation case. `Locale.ROOT` for that reason: this is the invariant machine form, not a presentation choice.

  **Test failures get better, not worse.** `expected "4.80 EC" but was "5.00 EC"` is the comparison a reader wants; `Ethecoin[minorUnits=480]` makes them do the division. ⚠ **Nothing serialises through `toString`** — the wire form is `minorUnits()`, and a test pins that it did not move.

  ⚠ **`Cycles` got the same treatment with no leak found.** It is the identical shape — a value wrapper over a `long`, reached everywhere via its accessor — and every call site is currently correct only because somebody happened to reach for `.cycles()` each time. The cost of being wrong is a player reading `Cycles[cycles=12]`; the cost of being right is four lines.

- 2026-07-30 — **Thirteen money formatters collapsed into one, and a shipped sign bug fixed with them** — recorded in `Ethecoin.format(long)` and `EthecoinTest.oneCanonicalFormatter`.

  **The cleanup was supposed to be tidying. It was not.** Twelve of the thirteen private `money(long)` copies were written as `String.format("%d.%02d EC", minorUnits / 100, Math.abs(minorUnits % 100))`. Java's integer division truncates **toward zero**, so for any amount between −1 and −99 the whole part is `0`, and `-0` is `0` — **the minus sign vanished**. Measured: `-5` → `0.05 EC`, `-50` → `0.50 EC`, `-99` → `0.99 EC`; correct from −100 down.

  ⚠ **Every transaction fee in the game is 2, 6 or 30 minor units** (`FEE_ECONOMY/STANDARD/PRIORITY`), so **every fee row in the LEDGER displayed as a credit** — on the one window whose entire job is showing money move. The thirteenth copy, `BalanceReadout`, used `%.2f` over a double and was accidentally correct, which is why the two never visibly disagreed on a balance.

  **`format` takes a `long`, not an `Ethecoin`, and that seam is deliberate.** An `Ethecoin` is never negative by construction — a balance does not go below zero, and affordability is asked before the subtraction rather than discovered from a sign. A ledger **delta** is signed and still has to render. So the value type stays non-negative and the formatter accepts the signed quantity the ledger actually holds.

  ⚠ **`Math.abs` is applied to the whole part, never to `minorUnits` itself**, so `Long.MIN_VALUE` cannot overflow into a negative magnitude — unreachable from play, reachable from a hand-edited save, and a formatter that throws takes down the window trying to draw it. Tested.

  ⚠ **`EthecoinTest.noDisplayFormatting` asserted the opposite and was rewritten.** It required that no `format` method exist, reasoning that *"a wire type that formats invites a second, subtly different formatter to appear on the server."* The risk was real and the conclusion backwards: what a type with **no** formatter invites is a private copy everywhere one is needed — thirteen of them, twelve subtly wrong. The surviving half of that guarantee is still asserted by reflection: **no `Locale`-taking overload**, because a localized amount is presentation and belongs to the client.

  **Verified end to end** rather than by unit test alone: a real purchase now writes `PURCHASE −8.00 EC` and `TX_FEE −0.06 EC` into the ledger.

- 2026-07-30 — **Ethecoin divides to 18 places, as ether does; the UI shows only significant decimals** — on explicit direction, with the cost surfaced and accepted before starting — recorded in `protocol/game/Ethecoin`, `Balance.ec(...)`, `GameSave.moneySchema` / `GameEngine.rescaleMoney`, and server migration `V2__core_schema.sql`.

  **The scale was a `[PROPOSAL]` of two decimal places**, chosen because two was the finest granularity any published figure used. What that missed is that a currency players *send each other* needs room for amounts nobody published — dust is a real thing to send. The smallest unit is now `1e-18` EC, the relationship wei has to ether.

  ⚠ **A `long` could not carry it, and that was measured before anything was changed.** At 18 decimals a `long` tops out at **9.22 EC** — less than one firmware image costs — so the obvious representation is not merely tight, it is unusable. `Ethecoin` therefore carries a `BigInteger`, and Postgres `bigint` (also int8) became `numeric(78,0)`.

  **Display shows only significant decimals.** A fixed `%.18f` would render every ordinary amount as `8.000000000000000000 EC` — eighteen characters of noise on every ledger row, burying the two amounts on screen that genuinely have a tail. ⚠ **Trimming is never rounding**: every significant digit prints however many there are, and what is dropped is only zeros that carry no information. ⚠ `stripTrailingZeros` leaves a **negative scale** — `500` becomes `5E+2` — so `toPlainString` is the guard; caught by rendering, and a test walks whole amounts to hold it.

  ⚠ **Ratios stay `double`; only amounts moved.** A payout fraction, a pool fee, a gas price and a buffer fill are pure ratios with no scale in them, and a double carries them exactly as well as anything else. What must never be a double is the wei amount they scale — past 2^53 (~0.009 EC) a double cannot hold an integer exactly, and at 18 places that residue lands squarely inside the digits the formatter now prints.

  ⚠ **NPC fees and transfers are drawn in hundredths and scaled up, not drawn across the wei range.** A uniform draw over 18 decimals gives every NPC transaction an eighteen-digit tail, and a block full of those reads as machine output rather than as people sending each other money. The representation being finer than the prices is the point of a fine representation, not a licence to use all of it.

  ⚠ **`rig.miningResidueWei` had to become a `BigDecimal`.** It carries the fraction of a unit a payout did not fill so that a hundred thirds bank thirty-three rather than nothing. A double held that fine at two decimal places; at 1e-18 EC the accumulated value passes a double's exact range inside one ordinary payout, and the residue would start *absorbing* rounding error instead of preventing it.

  **Save migration.** Gated on `GameSave.moneySchema`, absent-means-old, multiply by 10^16, runs exactly once, logged. ⚠ **No heuristic**: "is this balance suspiciously small?" is unanswerable — a player may legitimately hold 8 wei — and a migration that guesses eventually doubles somebody's balance. ⚠ `newCharacter` stamps the current schema, or a first launch would announce a migration that did not happen. ⚠ The `MISSING` fee sentinel is skipped, or "no fee recorded" becomes a large negative fee. Verified against a real pre-migration save, including a second load.

  ⚠ **The server columns were RENAMED, not just retyped.** `_ec_minor` meant integral hundredths; a column named that holding wei is a lie that outlives everyone who knew better — and the suffix is load-bearing, since `EconomyColumns` refuses to bind an ethecoin value through a column whose name does not carry it (Invariant **I1** made mechanical).
