Rendered and confirmed: after a deep scan and a shallow rescan nineteen hours later, the top two rungs read "1m ago" and the rest still read "19h ago".

  ⚠ **Findings merge; a shallow rescan erases nothing.** Replacing the report wholesale would throw away a deep scan's vault estimate the next time the player ran a cheap firewall check, which is exactly what would make paying for depth feel pointless.

  **`Sighting.reported` cleared the bar `NetTypesTest` guards**, which locks that record's field list by name so an addition is a deliberate edit rather than a renderer's convenience. It qualifies for the same reason `patched` did: it is not an observation of the machine — it says nothing about the far side — it is the player's own relationship to one, namely whether they have notes on it. It is deliberately boolean; how much is in the file is the report's own first line, and a column trying to carry completeness would need seven states in three characters.

  ⚠ **`NetText.STATE` widened 12 → 14**, because `foothold [i]` is exactly twelve characters and ran into the next column with no gap. `NetHostListTest` calls the widths a contract, so that was an edit in two places.

  **A real bug the tests found:** `PortScanRules` refused with "no machine that a sweep has found" while actually checking the **topology**, which contains every host in the world discovered or not — so the check was a check on nothing and a player could scan a machine they had never seen. It now requires `host.discovered`, which is what the refusal always claimed. Surfaced because a test could not assert on the sighting of a machine the map had never heard of.

- 2026-07-29 — **RECON became the reports; `port-scan(1)` shipped; node names and tags** — `client/view/ReconView`, `client/terms/en/1/port-scan.md`, `engine/net/NodeReports`.

  **The reference moved to the manual.** RECON opened as a cost model and two paragraphs on what a port scan teaches, because there was nothing collected to show. That belongs in `man port-scan`, which a player reads once and can find deliberately — a window that made them scroll past the same explanation every time they wanted a report would be teaching the explanation and hiding the data. `MoreViews.recon` is kept as a one-line pointer rather than deleted, so anything still reaching for the placeholder lands somewhere that says where the real one went.

  ⚠ **The page shipped as `port-scan(1)`, not `port-sweep(1)`, and that is a naming decision.** `docs/education/05` §3 reserves `port-scan(7)` and names the command page `port-sweep(1)`, following `design/glossary.md`'s recon tool "Port Sweep". But **this game already uses "sweep" for finding machines** — `net`'s sweep ladder, `SweepTier`, `NetRules.settleSweep` — and the outward interrogation of *one* machine is a different act. Shipping it as `port-sweep` would have collided with a live mechanic in the one vocabulary the teaching layer exists to keep straight. The distinction is now load-bearing and worth teaching: **a sweep finds machines; a port scan interrogates one.** Recorded in the page's `notes:`.

  ⚠ **`seeAlso` was rewritten from the spec's list**, which names `enumeration(7)`, `passive-sniffer(1)` and `ping-sweep(1)` — none of which exist. Every reference must resolve or the cross-reference check fails, so it points at the four pages that do.

  **Names and tags.** A machine with a file can be given a name and free-text tags, and the search matches address, player name, world label and tags — because the box's whole job is to find a report from whatever happens to be remembered about it. ⚠ **The address is always printed**, whatever a machine has been named: two machines called "backup" are one row twice otherwise, and the address is the field every other window keys on. ⚠ **Tags are lowercased, trimmed and de-duplicated** on the way in; `Rich` and `rich` as two tags is a search the player has to guess between. ⚠ **Only a machine with a file can be named** — otherwise RECON becomes a bookmark folder with the reports buried in it.

  **CI fix in the same pass.** `NodeMenuTest` failed the GitHub Actions Linux job with `UnsupportedOperationException: Unable to open DISPLAY`. ⚠ **It is the only JUnit test in the repo that starts the JavaFX toolkit** — every other file that touches FX is a `*Snapshot` main class run by hand, which is the convention it is the exception to. It now **aborts** (skips) rather than failing when the toolkit cannot start, and deliberately does not swallow-and-pass: a regression test reporting success without executing is the exact failure that same class already warns about. Running it in CI needs `xvfb-run` on the Linux job or Monocle on the test classpath; neither is wired up, and both are a larger decision than this test. **Worth doing** — the guard is currently protecting nothing in CI.

## Open — UI-7: the client repaints on a clock where it should react to events

**Raised 2026-07-29, not acted on.** Several panels keep themselves current by rebuilding on a one-second `Pulse` rather than by reacting to the thing that changed. That is polling dressed as rendering, and it has now produced two distinct defects in one week:

- **The file manager's transfer bar** painted once and froze, because it repainted only on `session.onChange` — and nothing about the *save* changes while a download runs. Fixed by adding a pulse, which is the polling patch rather than the cure.
- **RECON's report rows** could not host an inline editor at all until the refresh was split in two, because rebuilding the row list every second tears down an open `TextField` **mid-keystroke**. The fix — data-driven rebuild plus a `ticking` list for the ages, plus an `editing` flag that suppresses rebuilds — works, and is three mechanisms standing in for one missing one.

**The shape of the real fix.** Records and components should publish what changed, and views should subscribe to that rather than re-deriving everything on a timer. `GameSession.onChange` already exists but is a single undifferentiated "something happened" signal, so a view either ignores it or rebuilds the world; there is no way to say "this one report changed" or "this task advanced". Genuinely wall-clock-derived text (ages, countdowns) is the one thing that legitimately wants a tick, and even then it should be the *only* thing on it.

⚠ **Not scoped, not started.** It touches `GameSession`, every panel that currently pulses, and the `ticking`/`repaint` split several views have grown independently. Worth doing before more panels acquire editable fields — each one currently has to rediscover the same three workarounds — but it is a client-architecture change and wants its own pass rather than being smuggled into a feature.

**Update 2026-07-29 — the substrate now exists; the migration does not.** `client/.../events/` is a CloudEvents v1.0.2 bus over Spring's multicaster, and intents, refusals, window changes, task completions and chain advances all publish to it (see §3). That is the "publish what changed" half. ⚠ **No view has been migrated off its `Pulse`**, deliberately: the change that added the bus was required to have no observable effect, and rewriting the panels' refresh strategy is exactly an observable effect. The remaining work is unchanged in shape and is now much cheaper — a subscriber with a type prefix replaces a timer — but the events published today are still coarse (`intent` names the method, not the record it touched), so "this one report changed" needs finer subjects before RECON can drop its `editing` guard.
